We will also use the following direct consequence of functional calculus. If a self-adjoint multiplier sequence $T_j$ has $\left\lVert[T_j]\right\rVert\leq c$, then $$w_j=T_j-\mathop{\mathrm{clip}}_c(T_j)\in\mathcal I_\varphi,\qquad
 \left\lVert T_j-w_j\right\rVert\leq c.$$ If $T_j$ is block diagonal for a partition, so is $w_j$. This converts the quotient estimates below into explicit corrections.

<a id="poly:section"></a>
### Finite mixed polynomials and buffered pinning

We develop a rule for fixing one vertex’s color while controlling the total positive-root mass of an expected characteristic polynomial. The rule will be applied repeatedly in the measured paving argument; its constants must therefore be independent of the matrix size and of previous color choices. We first identify the rooted mass and the change caused by one pin, then prove the inequality that relates them.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
#### Stable polynomials and local spectral data

<a id="poly:definition"></a>
<a id="poly:deletion"></a>
Let $Q$ be a finite set, let $W=(W_{vw})_{v,w\in Q}$ be Hermitian with zero diagonal, and let $a_v\in\mathbb R$. At each $v$, independently choose a color in $\{1,\ldots,r\}$ with probabilities $t_{vi}\geq0$, $\sum_i t_{vi}=1$. The matrix $W_{\mathrm{pav}}$ retains the entries between vertices of the same color and sets all other entries to zero. Define $$\label{poly:definition}
 f_Q(Y)=\mathbb E\det\bigl(\mathop{\mathrm{diag}}(Y_v+a_v)_{v\in Q}-W_{\mathrm{pav}}\bigr),
 \qquad f_\varnothing=1.$$ All restrictions to subsets of $Q$ use the restricted matrix, penalties, and probabilities. The polynomial is real and multiaffine, its leading monomial is $\prod_{v\in Q}Y_v$, and $$\label{poly:deletion}
 \partial_{Y_v}f_Q=f_{Q\setminus\{v\}}.$$ We write $f_Q(z)$ when every variable is set equal to $z$. The uniform-probability paving polynomial and its determinant-derivative representation occur in <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[RL20](#ref-RL20), Theorem 1.6 and Proposition 1.7]; independent nonuniform distributions are also discussed in <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[RS21](#ref-RS21), remark following Lemma 24]. We give the identities and stability arguments in the form needed for local perturbations below.

<a id="stmt-lem-stability"></a>
<div id="lem:stability" class="lemma">

<a id="poly:differential-formula"></a>
**Lemma 6** (Stability). *Put $t_i^{1/2}=\mathop{\mathrm{diag}}(\sqrt{t_{vi}})_{v\in Q}$. Then $$\label{poly:differential-formula}
 f_Q(Y)=
 \left(\prod_{v\in Q}\frac{1}{r!}\partial_{Y_v}^{r-1}\right)
 \prod_{i=1}^r
 \det\bigl(\mathop{\mathrm{diag}}(Y+a)-r t_i^{1/2}Wt_i^{1/2}\bigr).$$ This identity remains valid for complex $t_{vi}$ with $\sum_i t_{vi}=1$, where the expectation means the finite algebraic sum with the corresponding product weights, and the same square root is used in both occurrences of $t_i^{1/2}$. The algebraic identity also holds with complex penalties and arbitrary complex off-diagonal entries of $W$, still with zero diagonal.*

*More generally, let $\mathcal H$ be an open half-plane. If the product of determinants on the right of <a href="#poly:differential-formula" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="poly:differential-formula">[poly:differential-formula]</a>, for every principal restriction, is nonzero whenever all its variables lie in $\mathcal H$, then the same holds for $f_Q$. In particular, for Hermitian $W$ and real probabilities and penalties, $f_Q$ is upper-half-plane stable and its univariate specialization is real-rooted.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-stability"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-stability</summary>

<a id="poly:cycle-weight"></a>
*Proof.* Expand each determinant by permutations. Since $W$ has zero diagonal, the off-diagonal terms are collections of disjoint nontrivial permutation cycles. Each such cycle $C$ has sign minus and weight $$\label{poly:cycle-weight}
 w_C=\left(\prod_{(v,w)\in C}W_{vw}\right)
 \sum_{i=1}^r\prod_{v\in C}t_{vi}$$ in the expectation; unused vertices contribute $Y_v+a_v$. Indeed, all vertices on a cycle must receive one common color, and disjoint cycles involve disjoint independent choices.

In the product on the right of <a href="#poly:differential-formula" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="poly:differential-formula">[poly:differential-formula]</a>, suppose a vertex occurs in nontrivial cycles of $k$ different determinant factors. Its degree in $Y_v+a_v$ is $r-k$. If $k\geq2$, the indicated derivative kills the term. If $k=0$, the normalized derivative sends $(Y_v+a_v)^r$ to $Y_v+a_v$. If $k=1$, it sends $(Y_v+a_v)^{r-1}$ to $1/r$, cancelling the factor $r$ from the matrix scaling at that vertex. The remaining terms are precisely <a href="#poly:cycle-weight" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="poly:cycle-weight">[poly:cycle-weight]</a> for disjoint cycles. The two square-root factors at each cycle vertex multiply to $t_{vi}$, so this argument also proves the complex-parameter identity independently of the choices of square roots. This cycle calculation is algebraic in the entries of $W$ and does not require them to be Hermitian; that hypothesis is used for the real-data stability conclusion, not for the identity.

For the half-plane assertion, proceed by induction on the subset $D$ of vertices at which $r-1$ derivatives have already been taken, simultaneously for all principal restrictions. Fix the other variables in $\mathcal H$ and take a vertex $v\notin D$. In each determinant, the coefficient of $Y_v$ is its principal determinant with $v$ removed, even for complex penalties. Extracting the coefficient of $Y_v^r$ from the product commutes with all normalized derivatives at vertices in $D$. Thus this coefficient is exactly the already-processed product for the principal set with $v$ removed. It is nonzero by induction, so the polynomial in $Y_v$ has degree exactly $r$. Its roots lie in the closed convex complement of $\mathcal H$. Gauss–Lucas implies that each of its successive derivatives has all roots in that complement and is nonzero. This proves the induction step.

<a id="poly:matrix-numerical-range"></a>
For completeness, the determinant hypothesis holds whenever $\mathcal H$ lies strictly beyond a numerical-range bound for $$\label{poly:matrix-numerical-range}
 B_i=r t_i^{1/2}Wt_i^{1/2}-\mathop{\mathrm{diag}}(a).$$ For example, if $\operatorname{Im}\left\langle B_i u,u\right\rangle\leq\beta\left\lVert u\right\rVert^2$ and $\operatorname{Im}Y_v>\beta$ for all $v$, a null vector of $\mathop{\mathrm{diag}}(Y)-B_i$ contradicts the imaginary part of its quadratic form. The same reasoning applies after a common rotation of the half-plane; principal compressions inherit numerical-range bounds. For Hermitian $W$ and real probabilities and penalties, the matrices <a href="#poly:matrix-numerical-range" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="poly:matrix-numerical-range">[poly:matrix-numerical-range]</a> are Hermitian, giving upper-half-plane stability. Real coefficients and complex conjugation then give real-rootedness of the univariate specialization. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-local-data"></a>
<div id="lem:local-data" class="lemma">

<a id="poly:pick-inequality"></a>
<a id="poly:arrow-quotient"></a>
<a id="poly:arrow-matrix"></a>
<a id="poly:stieltjes"></a>
<a id="poly:moments"></a>
**Lemma 7** (Local spectral data). *Fix $v\in Q$, put $f=f_Q$, $g=f_{Q\setminus\{v\}}$, and write $h=f/g$, $G=1/h$ at univariate arguments. For $z\in\mathbb C$ with $\operatorname{Im}z>0$, $$\label{poly:pick-inequality}
 \operatorname{Im}h(z)\geq\operatorname{Im}z.$$ If $s_j$ are the roots of $g$, with multiplicities, there are $\nu_j\geq0$ such that $$\label{poly:arrow-quotient}
 h(z)=z+a_v-\sum_j\frac{\nu_j}{z-s_j}.$$ Consequently, on choosing $|c_j|^2=\nu_j$, $$\label{poly:arrow-matrix}
 B=\begin{pmatrix}-a_v&c^*\\ c&S\end{pmatrix},
 \qquad S=\mathop{\mathrm{diag}}(s_j),
 \qquad \det(zI-B)=f(z).$$ The function $G$ is the transform $$\label{poly:stieltjes}
 G(z)=\int_\mathbb R\frac{d\gamma_v(x)}{z-x}$$ of the spectral probability measure of $B$ at its first coordinate. Its first two moments are $$\label{poly:moments}
 \begin{gathered}
 \int x\,d\gamma_v(x)=-a_v,
 \qquad
 \int x^2\,d\gamma_v(x)=a_v^2+\sum_j\nu_j,
 \\
 \sum_j\nu_j=\sum_{w\ne v}|W_{vw}|^2\sum_i t_{vi}t_{wi}.
 \end{gathered}$$*

<a id="poly:poisson"></a>
<a id="poly:sum-local-measures"></a>
*Let $N_f(x)$ and $N_g(x)$ count roots strictly greater than $x$, including multiplicities, and define <a id="poly:local-functionals"></a>$$\label{poly:local-functionals}
 \xi_h(x)=N_f(x)-N_g(x),\qquad
 \ell_v=\int_0^\infty\xi_h(x)\,dx,\qquad
 m_v=\int_\mathbb Rx_+\,d\gamma_v(x).$$ Then $\xi_h=\mathbf1_{\{h<0\}}$ off a finite subset of $\mathbb R$, and $$\label{poly:poisson}
 \frac{1}{\pi}\arg h(x+i\eta)
   =(P_\eta*\xi_h)(x),
 \qquad P_\eta(x)=\frac{\eta}{\pi(x^2+\eta^2)},\quad\eta>0,$$ where $\arg h\in(0,\pi)$. Moreover, $$\label{poly:sum-local-measures}
 \sum_{v\in Q}G_{v,Q}(z)=\frac{f'_Q(z)}{f_Q(z)},
 \qquad
 \sum_{v\in Q}\gamma_v=\sum_{f_Q(\lambda)=0}\delta_\lambda,$$ with multiplicities on the right.*

<a id="poly:pin-average"></a>
<a id="poly:buffered-quotient"></a>
*If $v$ is made deterministic of color $i$, write $h_i$ for the new quotient relative to the same $g$, and put $R_i=h_i/h$. Its representation has the same $a_v$ and poles $s_j$, and masses $\nu_{ij}\geq0$ which can be chosen so that $$\label{poly:pin-average}
 h=\sum_i t_{vi}h_i,\qquad
 \nu_j=\sum_i t_{vi}\nu_{ij}.$$ If the penalty at $v$ is also changed to $Ka_v$, the quotient becomes $$\label{poly:buffered-quotient}
 h_i'=h_i+(K-1)a_v.$$ Here the prime with a color index denotes the buffered quotient, not a derivative. We write $\ell'_{v,i}$ for the positive-axis integral of the spectral shift defined using $h_i'$ and the unchanged $g$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-local-data"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-local-data</summary>

*Proof.* Fix every variable except $Y_v$ at $z$ in the upper half-plane. By <a href="#poly:deletion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="poly:deletion">[poly:deletion]</a>, division by $g(z)\ne0$ produces a monic affine polynomial $Y_v-\alpha(z)$. Its root cannot be in the upper half-plane by Lemma <a href="#lem:stability" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:stability">6</a>. Hence $h(z)=z-\alpha(z)$ satisfies <a href="#poly:pick-inequality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="poly:pick-inequality">[poly:pick-inequality]</a>.

The rational function $h$ has real coefficients and only real poles. Its upper-half-plane property rules out poles of order two or higher: the imaginary part of a real leading polar term of such order has both signs along rays approaching the pole from above. At an actual simple pole the residue must be negative, as seen by approaching vertically. The polynomial part is $z+a_v$, by comparing the two highest coefficients of the monic polynomials $f$ and $g$. This gives <a href="#poly:arrow-quotient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="poly:arrow-quotient">[poly:arrow-quotient]</a>. For repeated roots of $g$, assign the total residue to one copy and pad with zero masses at the others. The Schur complement proves <a href="#poly:arrow-matrix" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="poly:arrow-matrix">[poly:arrow-matrix]</a> and <a href="#poly:stieltjes" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="poly:stieltjes">[poly:stieltjes]</a>, first away from the poles and then as identities of rational functions. The first two moments follow from the first diagonal entries of $B$ and $B^2$. Expanding $f/g$ at infinity, the coefficient of $1/z$ is minus the sum of the length-two cycle weights through $v$, giving the final identity in <a href="#poly:moments" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="poly:moments">[poly:moments]</a>.

For real $x$ away from roots and poles, the Schur complement of $xI-S$ in $xI-B$ is $h(x)$. Inertia is additive under this congruence, so the number of negative eigenvalues of $xI-B$ exceeds that of $xI-S$ by exactly $\mathbf1_{\{h(x)<0\}}$. This proves the assertion about $\xi_h$ and in particular $0\leq\xi_h\leq1$ almost everywhere.

Factor $f$ and $g$ over their real roots. On the upper half-plane, take each factor $z-\lambda$ with argument in $(0,\pi)$. The sum of the numerator arguments minus the denominator arguments equals $\arg h$: their difference is a continuous multiple of $2\pi$ and tends to zero at $z=iR$ as $R\to\infty$. The argument of $x+i\eta-\lambda$, divided by $\pi$, is the Poisson integral of $\mathbf1_{(-\infty,\lambda)}$. Summing and subtracting gives <a href="#poly:poisson" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="poly:poisson">[poly:poisson]</a>. Finally the chain rule and <a href="#poly:deletion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="poly:deletion">[poly:deletion]</a> give the first identity in <a href="#poly:sum-local-measures" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="poly:sum-local-measures">[poly:sum-local-measures]</a>. The second follows by comparing the finite Stieltjes transforms on the two sides.

Pinning changes only the probability at $v$, so $g$ is unchanged and $f$ is the weighted sum of its pinned versions. This gives the first identity in <a href="#poly:pin-average" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="poly:pin-average">[poly:pin-average]</a>. Each pinned polynomial is stable by Lemma <a href="#lem:stability" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:stability">6</a>, and thus has the same type of quotient representation. Comparing residues at each distinct pole gives the second identity; use the same placement on repeated copies for every choice. Finally changing the $v$-penalty adds $(K-1)a_v g$ to the polynomial, proving <a href="#poly:buffered-quotient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="poly:buffered-quotient">[poly:buffered-quotient]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="poly:cost-response"></a>
The two local quantities have different roles. Define the total positive-root mass, with roots counted according to multiplicity, by <a id="poly:total-cost"></a>$$\label{poly:total-cost}
 \mathcal E(Q)=\sum_{f_Q(\lambda)=0}\lambda_+.$$ The sum-of-measures identity and the definition of the spectral shift give $$\label{poly:cost-response}
 \mathcal E(Q)=\sum_{v\in Q}m_v,\qquad
 \ell_v=\mathcal E(Q)-\mathcal E(Q\setminus\{v\}).$$ Thus $m_v$ distributes the total cost among the vertices, whereas $\ell_v$ is the cost of inserting $v$. A buffered pin at $v$ leaves the deleted polynomial unchanged, so its change of total cost is exactly $\ell_{v,i}'-\ell_v$. These are costs of the expected characteristic polynomial; in general they are not expectations of the positive-part trace of the randomly paved matrix. They become the ordinary spectral costs when all colors are deterministic.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### The initial largest-root bound

The following estimate is obtained in <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[RS21](#ref-RS21), Lemma 24 and proof of Theorem 25]. We include a derivation from the MSS covariance bound, explaining both the positive-defect completion and the deletion of the added Gram columns.

<a id="stmt-lem-mss-paving"></a>
<div id="lem:mss-paving" class="lemma">

<a id="poly:mss-bound"></a>
**Lemma 8** (Initial largest-root bound). *Let $T$ be a Hermitian contraction on a nonempty finite index set $Q$, with zero diagonal. In <a href="#poly:definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="poly:definition">[poly:definition]</a>, take $W=T$, $a_v=0$, and $t_{vi}=1/r$. Then $$\label{poly:mss-bound}
 \max\operatorname{root}(f_Q)\leq2\sqrt{2/r}+2/r.$$ In particular, giving every vertex the same penalty $b$ places all roots strictly below zero if $2\sqrt{2/r}+2/r<b$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-mss-paving"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-mss-paving</summary>

*Proof.* Set $D_0=I+T$. Then $0\leq D_0\leq2I$ and $(D_0)_{vv}=1$. Independently form the columns $$u_v=D_0^{1/2}e_v\otimes e_{\mathrm{color}(v)}
       \quad\text{in }\mathbb C^Q\otimes\mathbb C^r.$$ They have squared norm one, their Gram matrix is $I+T_{\mathrm{pav}}$, and, with $d=2/r$, $$\sum_{v\in Q}\mathbb Eu_vu_v^*=(D_0/r)\otimes I_r\leq dI.$$ Write the positive defect as a finite sum of deterministic outer products. Splitting its positive eigenvalues into finitely many summands at most one makes each added column have squared norm at most one. For all original and added columns together, their covariances $A_j$ satisfy $\sum_j A_j=dI$ and $\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}A_j\leq1$.

<a id="poly:enlarged-root"></a>
Theorem 4.1 of <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[MSS15](#ref-MSS15)] identifies the expected characteristic polynomial of a sum of independent, finitely supported rank-one outer products with the mixed characteristic polynomial of the covariances. Theorem 5.1 of <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[MSS15](#ref-MSS15)] gives largest root at most $(1+\sqrt{\epsilon})^2$ for positive semidefinite covariances summing to $I$ with individual traces at most $\epsilon$. Apply these results to $A_j/d$, with $\epsilon=1/d$, and rescale the spectral variable. The expected characteristic polynomial of the enlarged sum has largest root at most $$\label{poly:enlarged-root}
 d(1+\sqrt{1/d})^2=1+2\sqrt d+d.$$

<a id="poly:gram-stability"></a>
We justify carefully the removal of the added columns. For independent, finitely supported columns $u_1,\ldots,u_m$ in a finite-dimensional space, let $A_j=\mathbb Eu_ju_j^*$ and let $U$ be the matrix of columns. Then $$\label{poly:gram-stability}
 F(Y):=\mathbb E\det\bigl(\mathop{\mathrm{diag}}(Y)-U^*U\bigr)
 =\left.
   \left(\prod_{j=1}^m(Y_j-\partial_{s_j})\right)
   \det\left(I+\sum_{j=1}^m s_j A_j\right)
   \right|_{s=0}.$$ Indeed, the coefficient indexed by a subset $J$ of columns on either side is $(-1)^{|J|}$ times the expected principal Gram determinant $\mathbb E\det(U_J^*U_J)$. This follows first for deterministic rank-one matrices by determinant expansion, and then for the mean outer products by independence and multilinearity in the distinct matrices indexed by $J$.

The determinant on the right of <a href="#poly:gram-stability" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="poly:gram-stability">[poly:gram-stability]</a> is stable in the variables $s_j$. If it had a null vector with all $\operatorname{Im}s_j>0$, taking the imaginary part of its quadratic form would force the vector into $\ker A_j$ for every $j$; the identity term then forces the vector to be zero. If a polynomial $p$ is stable, fixing all variables except $s_j$ at upper-half-plane arguments gives $$\frac{\partial_{s_j}p}{p}=
 \sum_k\frac{1}{s_j-\lambda_k},\qquad
 \operatorname{Im}\lambda_k\leq0.$$ The sum is zero when the degree is zero, and otherwise has negative imaginary part. Thus a new upper-half-plane variable $Y_j$ cannot equal this logarithmic derivative, and $(Y_j-\partial_{s_j})p$ is stable. Repeating this observation, and then specializing the $s_j$ to zero by the zero-free limit theorem, proves stability of $F$. The limit is not the zero polynomial, since its leading monomial $\prod_jY_j$ has coefficient one. The same proof works for every principal set of columns.

Differentiating $F$ in a diagonal variable deletes the corresponding column from the expected Gram polynomial. The monic affine argument of Lemma <a href="#lem:local-data" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:local-data">7</a> therefore applies to the quotient of its univariate specialization by the deleted polynomial. It has imaginary part at least $\operatorname{Im}z$, and its polynomial part is $z-\mathbb E\left\lVert u_j\right\rVert^2$. The residue argument in that Lemma realizes the full polynomial as the characteristic polynomial of a Hermitian arrow matrix whose principal compression has the deleted polynomial as characteristic polynomial. The largest eigenvalue of a principal compression cannot exceed the largest eigenvalue of the whole matrix. Thus deleting columns cannot increase the largest root.

If the ambient column space has dimension $q$, the exact identity $$z^q\mathbb E\det(zI_m-U^*U)
   =z^m\mathbb E\det(zI_q-UU^*)$$ shows that the enlarged Gram polynomial and the enlarged sum polynomial differ only by zero roots. Since the bound <a href="#poly:enlarged-root" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="poly:enlarged-root">[poly:enlarged-root]</a> is positive, it also bounds the largest root of the enlarged Gram polynomial. Delete every added column using the preceding paragraph. The original Gram polynomial is $\mathbb E\det(zI-(I+T_{\mathrm{pav}}))=f_Q(z-1)$. Shifting by minus one proves <a href="#poly:mss-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="poly:mss-bound">[poly:mss-bound]</a>. A common penalty $b$ replaces $f_Q(z)$ by $f_Q(z+b)$, giving the final assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### Residue tails and exceedance lengths

<a id="poly:general-arrow"></a>
The rest of this section concerns arbitrary arrow quotients, not just those arising from a coloring. Let $s_j\in\mathbb R$, $\nu_j\geq0$, and $$\label{poly:general-arrow}
 h(z)=z+a-\sum_j\frac{\nu_j}{z-s_j},\qquad a>0.$$ Let $\gamma$ be the first-coordinate spectral probability measure of the corresponding arrow matrix, and put <a id="poly:length-definition"></a>$$\label{poly:length-definition}
 m=\int x_+\,d\gamma(x),\qquad
 L(a,\nu)=\left|\left\{\lambda>0:
       \sum_j\frac{\nu_j}{\lambda-s_j}>\lambda+a\right\}\right|.$$ Here and below $|\cdot|$ denotes Lebesgue measure when applied to a set of real numbers. By Lemma <a href="#lem:local-data" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:local-data">7</a>, this length is the positive-axis integral of the spectral shift for $h$.

<a id="stmt-poly-tail-control"></a>
<div id="poly:tail-control" class="lemma">

<a id="poly:near-mass"></a>
<a id="poly:positive-tail"></a>
<a id="poly:negative-tail"></a>
**Lemma 9** (Control of the residues). *Suppose $a=1$ in <a href="#poly:general-arrow" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="poly:general-arrow">[poly:general-arrow]</a>, and set $H=\int|x|\,d\gamma(x)=1+2m$. There is a universal $C_0$ such that $$\begin{aligned}
 \sum_{|s_j|\leq60H}\nu_j&\leq C_0H^2,
       \label{poly:near-mass}\\
 \sum_{s_j\geq20H}\frac{\nu_j}{s_j}&\leq C_0m,
       \label{poly:positive-tail}\\
 \sum_{s_j\leq-20H}\frac{\nu_j}{|s_j|}&\leq C_0H.
       \label{poly:negative-tail}
\end{aligned}$$ For example, $C_0=6000$ suffices.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-poly-tail-control"><summary>Proof of stmt-poly-tail-control</summary>

*Proof.* The first spectral moment is $-1$, so $H=1+2m\geq1$. By Markov’s inequality, $$\gamma([-4H,4H])\geq3/4.$$ Writing $G=1/h$, we obtain $$-\operatorname{Im}G(i10H)
 =\int\frac{10H}{x^2+100H^2}\,d\gamma(x)
 \geq\frac{15}{232H}.$$ Since $\operatorname{Im}(1/G)\leq1/(-\operatorname{Im}G)$, $$10H+\sum_j\frac{10H\nu_j}{s_j^2+100H^2}
 =\operatorname{Im}h(i10H)\leq\frac{232}{15}H<16H.$$ For $|s_j|\leq60H$, the denominator is at most $3700H^2$. It follows that the left side of <a href="#poly:near-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="poly:near-mass">[poly:near-mass]</a> is at most $2220H^2$.

Let $e$ be the first coordinate vector of the arrow matrix $B$, and let $e_j$ be the other coordinate vectors, so that $(B-s_j)e_j$ is a scalar multiple of $e$ of modulus $\sqrt{\nu_j}$. Fix $\lambda\geq4H$ and $s_j\geq2\lambda$. Spectral calculus on $(-\infty,\lambda]$, which is separated from $s_j$, and $\left\langle e_j,e\right\rangle=0$ give $$\begin{aligned}
 \left|\left\langle e_j,\mathbf 1_{(\lambda,\infty)}(B)e\right\rangle\right|
 &=\left|\left\langle e_j,\mathbf 1_{(-\infty,\lambda]}(B)e\right\rangle\right|\\
 &=\sqrt{\nu_j}\int_{x\leq\lambda}\frac{d\gamma(x)}{s_j-x}
 \geq\frac{\sqrt{\nu_j}}{2s_j}.
\end{aligned}$$ The last inequality uses $\gamma([-\lambda,\lambda])\geq3/4$ and $s_j+\lambda\leq3s_j/2$. Bessel’s inequality now yields $$\sum_{s_j\geq2\lambda}\frac{\nu_j}{s_j^2}
 \leq4\gamma((\lambda,\infty)).$$ Integrating for $\lambda\geq4H$ gives $$\sum_{s_j\geq8H}\frac{\nu_j}{s_j^2}
                    \left(\frac{s_j}{2}-4H\right)
 \leq4\int_{4H}^\infty\gamma((\lambda,\infty))\,d\lambda
 \leq4m.$$ For $s_j\geq20H$ the summand on the left is at least $3\nu_j/(10s_j)$. Thus <a href="#poly:positive-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="poly:positive-tail">[poly:positive-tail]</a> holds with constant $40/3$. The same positive-tail calculation applied to $-B$ has first absolute moment $H$ and positive first moment $\int(-x)_+\,d\gamma(x)=1+m\leq H$. It gives <a href="#poly:negative-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="poly:negative-tail">[poly:negative-tail]</a> with the same constant. These calculations include zero residues and repeated poles. ◻

</details>

The next lemma is Boole’s identity for finite positive atomic measures <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[Boole1857](#ref-Boole1857)]; see <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[AW15](#ref-AW15), Proposition 2.6 and Appendix A]. For this one-sided normalization, see <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[PSZ09](#ref-PSZ09), Section 1, equations (1.1), (1.3), and (1.5)]. Its elementary root-sum proof will also fix the sign and endpoint conventions used here.

<a id="stmt-poly-boole"></a>
<div id="poly:boole" class="lemma">

**Lemma 10** (Boole length identity). *For finitely many real poles $s_j$, nonnegative weights $w_j$, and $c>0$, <a id="poly:boole-identities"></a>$$\label{poly:boole-identities}
 \left|\left\{\lambda\in\mathbb R:
       \sum_j\frac{w_j}{\lambda-s_j}>c\right\}\right|
 =\left|\left\{\lambda\in\mathbb R:
       \sum_j\frac{w_j}{\lambda-s_j}<-c\right\}\right|
 =\frac{\sum_jw_j}{c}.$$ If every pole carrying positive weight is strictly positive, the first exceedance set lies in $(0,\infty)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-poly-boole"><summary>Proof of stmt-poly-boole</summary>

*Proof.* Merge coincident poles and omit zero weights. If there are no remaining poles the assertion is immediate. Otherwise list them in increasing order. The rational function is strictly decreasing between poles. Immediately to the right of each pole it tends to $+\infty$; before the next pole it tends to $-\infty$, and after the last pole it tends to zero. Thus the positive-level exceedance consists of one interval from each pole to the ensuing root of the level equation. Clearing denominators in $\sum_jw_j/(\lambda-s_j)=c$, comparison of the two highest coefficients shows that the sum of the level roots exceeds the sum of the poles by $(\sum_jw_j)/c$. This is precisely the total interval length. Reflection of the variable gives the negative-level identity. With positive poles the rational function is nonpositive on $(-\infty,0]$, proving the last assertion. Values at the finitely many poles and endpoints do not affect these lengths. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### The buffered choice

The buffer makes it possible to retain coefficient one on the existing cost $\ell$. When $m$ is large, we bound the whole child cost by a multiple of $m$; when $m$ is small, we reserve half of the existing length and control the remaining exceedance by the residue tails.

<a id="stmt-lem-buffered-pin"></a>
<div id="lem:buffered-pin" class="lemma">

<a id="poly:buffered-pin-bound"></a>
**Lemma 11** (Buffered pin inequality). *There are universal constants $K\geq1$ and $C>0$ with the following property. In <a href="#poly:general-arrow" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="poly:general-arrow">[poly:general-arrow]</a>, let a finite family of choices have masses $\nu_{ij}\geq0$ and probabilities $t_i\geq0$ such that $\sum_i t_i=1$ and $\sum_i t_i\nu_{ij}=\nu_j$. Keep the poles $s_j$ unchanged, and define $$h_i'(z)=z+Ka-\sum_j\frac{\nu_{ij}}{z-s_j},
 \qquad \ell_i'=L(Ka,\nu_i),\qquad \ell=L(a,\nu).$$ Then $$\label{poly:buffered-pin-bound}
 \min_{i:t_i>0}\ell_i'\leq\ell+Cm.$$ The constants have no dependence on the number or positions of the poles, the masses, the probabilities, or the positive penalty $a$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-buffered-pin"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-buffered-pin</summary>

*Proof.* Dividing the arrow matrix by $a$ sends $s_j$ to $s_j/a$, the masses to $\nu_j/a^2$, and both lengths and $m$ to their values divided by $a$. It therefore suffices to prove the assertion for $a=1$. Write $L=L(1,\nu)$ and $H=1+2m$, and fix $C_0$ from Lemma <a href="#poly:tail-control" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="poly:tail-control">9</a>. Only positive-probability choices are used below.

<a id="poly:large-m-selection"></a>
<a id="poly:large-m-transform"></a>
<a id="poly:large-m-conclusion"></a>
Suppose first that $m\geq1$. The means of the two nonnegative statistics $$A_i=\sum_{|s_j|\leq20H}\nu_{ij},\qquad
 B_i=\sum_{|s_j|>20H}\frac{\nu_{ij}}{|s_j|}$$ are at most $C_0H^2$ and $C_0(m+H)\leq2C_0H$, respectively. Markov’s inequality and a union bound give a choice satisfying $$\label{poly:large-m-selection}
 A_i\leq C_2H^2,\qquad B_i\leq C_2H,
 \qquad C_2=8C_0.$$ For $\lambda>DH$ and $D\geq40$, all denominators from poles $|s_j|\leq20H$ are positive and their total contribution is at most $C_2H^2/(\lambda-20H)\leq C_2H$. The contribution from poles $s_j<-20H$ is at most $C_2H$ as well. Put $C_3=2C_2$. For the remaining positive poles the identity $$\label{poly:large-m-transform}
 \sum_{s_j>20H}\frac{\nu_{ij}}{\lambda-s_j}
 = (\lambda+H)\sum_{s_j>20H}
       \frac{\nu_{ij}}{(s_j+H)(\lambda-s_j)}
       -\sum_{s_j>20H}\frac{\nu_{ij}}{s_j+H}$$ holds. Choose $D\geq\max\{40,2C_3+1\}$. For any $K\geq1$, if the entire rational sum exceeds $\lambda+K$, then <a href="#poly:large-m-transform" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="poly:large-m-transform">[poly:large-m-transform]</a> forces $$\sum_{s_j>20H}\frac{\nu_{ij}}{(s_j+H)(\lambda-s_j)}>\frac12.$$ Indeed the necessary lower bound for this sum is at least $(\lambda-C_3H)/(\lambda+H)\geq1/2$. By Lemma <a href="#poly:boole" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="poly:boole">10</a> and <a href="#poly:large-m-selection" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="poly:large-m-selection">[poly:large-m-selection]</a>, its exceedance length is at most $$2\sum_{s_j>20H}\frac{\nu_{ij}}{s_j+H}\leq2C_2H.$$ Including the initial interval $(0,DH)$, we obtain $$\label{poly:large-m-conclusion}
 L(K,\nu_i)\leq(D+2C_2)H\leq3(D+2C_2)m.$$ This proves the required bound in this case, with a universal constant, for every $K\geq1$.

<a id="poly:parent-exceedance"></a>
<a id="poly:P-controlled-by-L"></a>
It remains to treat $0\leq m<1$, so $1\leq H\leq3$. Set $$P=\sum_{s_j>0}\frac{\nu_j}{1+s_j},\qquad
 M_-(d)=\sum_{s_j\leq0}\frac{\nu_j}{d+|s_j|}\quad(d>0),$$ using the right limit $M_-(0+)$ when necessary. Splitting the positive poles at $60$ and using Lemma <a href="#poly:tail-control" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="poly:tail-control">9</a> gives <a id="poly:P-bound"></a>$$\label{poly:P-bound}
 P\leq9C_0+C_0m\leq C_4,
 \qquad C_4=10C_0.$$ Here $60\leq60H$ controls the near part and $60\geq20H$ controls the tail. For $\lambda>0$ away from the poles, the parent exceedance condition is equivalent to $$\label{poly:parent-exceedance}
 R(\lambda):=\sum_{s_j>0}
 \frac{\nu_j}{(1+s_j)(\lambda-s_j)}
 >1+\frac{P-M_-(\lambda)}{1+\lambda}.$$ The threshold on the right is at most $1+P$. All poles of $R$ with positive weight are positive. Lemma <a href="#poly:boole" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="poly:boole">10</a> therefore gives $$\label{poly:P-controlled-by-L}
 L\geq\frac{P}{1+P},\qquad
 P\leq C_5L,\qquad C_5=1+C_4.$$

<a id="poly:negative-control"></a>
We next prove the uniform bound $$\label{poly:negative-control}
 M_-(L/2)\leq C_6,$$ with the right-limit interpretation when $L=0$. If $L\geq1$, split the negative poles at absolute value $60H$. The near part is at most $2C_0H^2\leq18C_0$, while the far part is at most $C_0H\leq3C_0$. Thus $21C_0$ suffices in this case. If $L=0$, then <a href="#poly:P-controlled-by-L" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="poly:P-controlled-by-L">[poly:P-controlled-by-L]</a> implies $P=0$. If $M_-(0+)>1$, including an infinite right limit, then $M_-(\lambda)>1+\lambda$ on a nonempty interval immediately to the right of zero. With no positive-pole contribution this would be an interval of parent exceedance, contradicting $L=0$. Hence $M_-(0+)\leq1$.

Finally suppose $0<L<1$. If $M_-(2L)>7C_5+9$, then for $0<\lambda<2L$ we have $P\leq C_5$, $M_-(\lambda)\geq M_-(2L)$, and $1+\lambda\leq3$. Consequently the right side of <a href="#poly:parent-exceedance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="poly:parent-exceedance">[poly:parent-exceedance]</a> is strictly less than $-2(C_5+1)$. By the negative-level identity in Lemma <a href="#poly:boole" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="poly:boole">10</a>, $$\left|\{\lambda\in\mathbb R:R(\lambda)<-2(C_5+1)\}\right|
 =\frac{P}{2(C_5+1)}<\frac L2.$$ Outside this exceptional set, the parent exceedance holds throughout $(0,2L)$, apart from finitely many poles. Its length is therefore greater than $3L/2$, contradicting the definition of $L$. It follows that $M_-(2L)\leq7C_5+9$. Termwise comparison of denominators gives $M_-(L/2)\leq4M_-(2L)$. Thus <a href="#poly:negative-control" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="poly:negative-control">[poly:negative-control]</a> holds in all cases with, for instance, <a id="poly:C6-choice"></a>$$\label{poly:C6-choice}
 C_6=\max\{21C_0,1,28C_5+36\}.$$

For each choice define $P_i$ and $M_{-,i}$ by replacing $\nu$ with $\nu_i$. Averaging these nonnegative quantities, followed by Markov’s inequality and a union bound, gives a single choice $i$ with <a id="poly:small-m-selection"></a>$$\label{poly:small-m-selection}
 P_i\leq4P,\qquad
 M_{-,i}(L/2)\leq4C_6,\qquad
 \sum_{s_j>60}\frac{\nu_{ij}}{s_j}\leq4C_0m.$$ There is no exception when one of the means is zero: the corresponding nonnegative statistic then vanishes at every positive-probability choice. When $L=0$, finiteness of $M_-(0+)$ forces $\nu_j=0$ at every pole $s_j=0$. The nonnegative averaging identities then force $\nu_{ij}=0$ at those poles for every positive-probability choice. The right-limit child statistics are therefore finite sums over strictly negative poles, and their averages equal the parent statistic. Thus the same selection argument applies at $L=0$ and at $m=0$.

<a id="poly:child-exceedance"></a>
<a id="poly:child-length-estimate"></a>
For this choice put $P_i^{(K)}=\sum_{s_j>0}\nu_{ij}/(K+s_j)$. The child’s exceedance condition is $$\label{poly:child-exceedance}
 \sum_{s_j>0}\frac{\nu_{ij}}{(K+s_j)(\lambda-s_j)}
 >1+\frac{P_i^{(K)}-M_{-,i}(\lambda)}{K+\lambda}.$$ For $\lambda>L/2$ the negative statistic is at most $4C_6$ by monotonicity. Therefore $K\geq8C_6$ makes the right side of <a href="#poly:child-exceedance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="poly:child-exceedance">[poly:child-exceedance]</a> at least $1/2$. The length of the child exceedance beyond $L/2$ is at most $2P_i^{(K)}$ by Lemma <a href="#poly:boole" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="poly:boole">10</a>. Split the positive poles at $60$. For $0<s_j\leq60$, $$\frac{1}{K+s_j}\leq\frac{61}{K}\frac{1}{1+s_j},$$ whereas for $s_j>60$ it is at most $1/s_j$. Thus $$\label{poly:child-length-estimate}
 2P_i^{(K)}
 \leq\frac{122}{K}P_i
       +2\sum_{s_j>60}\frac{\nu_{ij}}{s_j}
 \leq\frac{488C_5}{K}L+8C_0m.$$ Choose once and for all $$K\geq\max\{1,8C_6,976C_5\}.$$ The first term in <a href="#poly:child-length-estimate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="poly:child-length-estimate">[poly:child-length-estimate]</a> is at most $L/2$. Adding the possible length $L/2$ of the initial interval gives $L(K,\nu_i)\leq L+8C_0m$. Together with <a href="#poly:large-m-conclusion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="poly:large-m-conclusion">[poly:large-m-conclusion]</a>, this proves <a href="#poly:buffered-pin-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="poly:buffered-pin-bound">[poly:buffered-pin-bound]</a> with $C\geq\max\{8C_0,3(D+2C_2)\}$. Scaling back to arbitrary $a>0$ completes the proof. ◻

</details>

We fix the universal constants $K,C$ supplied by Lemma <a href="#lem:buffered-pin" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:buffered-pin">11</a> for the remainder of the argument. By <a href="#poly:cost-response" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="poly:cost-response">[poly:cost-response]</a>, at a vertex with positive penalty the lemma supplies a buffered pin increasing $\mathcal E(Q)$ by at most $Cm_v$. The coefficient one on $\ell_v$ is what cancels the old insertion cost in this difference. The measured argument will sum these local charges while making interactions between simultaneous pins negligible.

<a id="sec:slabs"></a>
### Measured matrices and coherent choices on Maharam slabs

<a id="slab:cocycle"></a>
Let $\mathcal R$ be a countable nonsingular Borel equivalence relation on a standard probability space $(V,\mu)$. All measurable objects are understood modulo null sets, with Borel representatives. Our convention for the multiplicative Radon–Nikodym cocycle is $$d\mu(w)=\omega(v,w)\,d\mu(v)
 \label{slab:cocycle}$$ along partial isomorphisms $v\mapsto w$ with graph in $\mathcal R$. An invariant null set can be removed so that the cocycle identities hold on the resulting relation.

<a id="slab:edge-cocycle"></a>
Consider a measurable system $T$ of Hermitian contractions with zero diagonal on the spaces $\ell^2([v]_{\mathcal R})$. The system is *principal*: the indices are the points of an equivalence class, with one index for each point. On changing the root within a class, the matrices agree up to conjugation by diagonal unitaries. Suppose their entries are supported on an undirected Borel graph $\mathcal G$ in $\mathcal R$, of degree at most a fixed finite number $D$, and suppose that for a fixed $J\geq1$, $$J^{-1}\leq\omega(v,w)\leq J
 \quad\text{on every edge of }\mathcal G.
 \label{slab:edge-cocycle}$$ A measurable coloring of $V$ defines $T_{\mathrm{pav}}$ by retaining entries whose two indices have the same color and setting the other entries to zero. For a function of an orbit matrix, its diagonal entry at the root $v$ is denoted by a subscript $vv$.

<a id="stmt-thm-measured-paving"></a>
<div id="thm:measured-paving" class="theorem">

<a id="slab:r-choice"></a>
**Theorem 12** (One-sided measured paving). *There is a universal constant $K\geq1$ with the following property. Suppose $T$, $\mathcal R$, $\mathcal G$, and $\omega$ satisfy the preceding assumptions. Let $b>0$, and choose a positive integer $r$ such that $$2\sqrt{2/r}+2/r<b.
 \label{slab:r-choice}$$ For every $\sigma>0$ and $\rho>0$, there is a measurable coloring of $V$ with $r+1$ colors for which <a id="slab:measured-conclusion"></a>$$\int_V \bigl((T_{\mathrm{pav}}-(Kb+\sigma)\mathbf 1)_+^2\bigr)_{vv}
 \,d\mu(v)<\rho.
 \label{slab:measured-conclusion}$$*

</div>

We use the constants $K$ and $C$ of Lemma <a href="#lem:buffered-pin" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:buffered-pin">11</a>. This section constructs choices that are coherent along the height coordinate of the Maharam extension: the finite buffered inequality selects a color at a lifted vertex, but a paving of the base requires the same choice at every height above that vertex. The proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:measured-paving" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:measured-paving">12</a> is completed by the sparse pinning and descent argument in the next section. All constants called uniform in this section may depend on $D,J,r,b$ and the fixed matrix system, but not on a partial assignment or on the endpoints of a slab. Constants for a single fixed slab will be identified separately.

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### Invariant measure and weighted polynomial systems

<a id="slab:mass-transport"></a>
For the Maharam extension, see <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[Maharam64](#ref-Maharam64)] and the formula in <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[RoySamorodnitsky06](#ref-RoySamorodnitsky06), Section 2, before Theorem 2.1]; the multiplicative coordinate there is $y=e^{-s}$ in our convention. We verify invariance in the present cocycle notation before developing the slab construction. The Maharam extension has vertices $u=(v,s)\in V\times\mathbb R$ and relates $(v,s)$ to $$(w,s+\log\omega(v,w)),\qquad (v,w)\in\mathcal R.$$ Projection to $V$ is injective on each lifted orbit: the cocycle identities fix the height over $w$ uniquely. The measure $$d\widetilde\mu(v,s)=d\mu(v)e^{-s}\,ds$$ is invariant along this relation. Indeed, writing $s'=s+\log\omega(v,w)$, Equation <a href="#slab:cocycle" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="slab:cocycle">[slab:cocycle]</a> gives $$d\mu(w)e^{-s'}ds'=\omega(v,w)d\mu(v)
       \frac{e^{-s}}{\omega(v,w)}\,ds=d\mu(v)e^{-s}ds.$$ Consequently the mass-transport identity is $$\int \sum_y F(u,y)\,d\widetilde\mu(u)
 =\int \sum_u F(u,y)\,d\widetilde\mu(y)
 \label{slab:mass-transport}$$ for nonnegative measurable transports along the lifted relation, and for integrable complex transports. The identity remains true after restricting both endpoints to a slab or to any further measurable subset. To justify it, decompose the countable Borel relation into countably many graphs of partial Borel isomorphisms, using the countable-section decomposition theorem and its application to the inverse relation. On each lifted graph it is precisely the preceding change of variables; summing the graphwise identities proves the claim. The scalar polynomial data used below are invariant under the allowed diagonal-unitary gauges, so they define transports without any choice of gauge.

Fix a bounded interval $I$ and restrict the lifted graph and matrices to $V\times I$. A partial assignment on the base $V$ specifies a color at some vertices. At unassigned vertices let $t_{vi}=1/r$ for every $i$ and let $c_v=b$; at assigned vertices let $(t_{vi})_i$ be the corresponding point mass and let $c_v=Kb$. Lift these data unchanged in $s$, and set <a id="slab:weighted-system"></a>$$d_u=e^s\quad(u=(v,s)),\qquad
 W=d^{1/2}Td^{1/2},\qquad a_u=d_uc_v.
 \label{slab:weighted-system}$$ The weight $d_u=e^s$ cancels the density $e^{-s}$ of the invariant measure: an absolute spectral cost integrated against $d\widetilde\mu$ is its normalized cost, divided by $d_u$, integrated against $d\mu\,ds$. This will make long slabs comparable by ordinary averages over height. We use the finite mixed polynomials and local data of Lemma <a href="#lem:local-data" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:local-data">7</a> with these matrices and penalties. A prospective buffered pin at an unassigned lifted vertex fixes its color and replaces its penalty $bd_u$ by $Kbd_u$. During comparisons we also allow arbitrary vertex deletions and single lifted-vertex changes; these intermediate configurations need not arise from a base assignment. Distances are always taken in the original restricted lift of $\mathcal G$, including edges whose entries have subsequently been set to zero.

All finite principal systems in a fixed slab, and all their prospective pins, have their roots and poles in a common interval $[-B,B]$. For example, an enlarged value of $\sup_u a_u+r\left\lVert W\right\rVert$ is sufficient: apply Lemma <a href="#lem:stability" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:stability">6</a> to the two half-planes beyond the numerical ranges of the determinant matrices. The same conclusion holds if some cross edges are cut or interpolated as below, with an enlarged fixed-slab bound.

<a id="slab:local-bounds"></a>
There are also estimates which do not depend on the slab. The residue formula in Lemma <a href="#lem:local-data" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:local-data">7</a>, together with $d_y/d_u\leq J$ on edges, gives $$a_u\leq Kbd_u,\qquad
 \sum_j\nu_{u,j}
 \leq \sum_y\left|W_{uy}\right|^2
 \leq Jd_u^2\sum_y\left|T_{uy}\right|^2
 \leq Jd_u^2.$$ They hold for each pin alternative as well. They continue to hold under the cuts and real edge interpolations used later, since these only decrease the absolute values of the entries. The second-moment formula for $\gamma_u$ and the arrow representation therefore give a constant $C_{\mathrm{loc}}$ such that $$m_u\leq C_{\mathrm{loc}}d_u,\qquad
 0\leq\ell_u\leq C_{\mathrm{loc}}d_u,\qquad
 0\leq\ell_{u,i}'\leq C_{\mathrm{loc}}d_u.
 \label{slab:local-bounds}$$ For the last two estimates, adding the off-diagonal arrow to $(-a_u)\oplus S$ changes the trace of the positive part by at most the trace norm of that arrow, namely $2(\sum_j\nu_{u,j})^{1/2}$. The positive-part trace variational formula proves this estimate; interlacing gives its nonnegative sign. Here and below $h_i'$ denotes the buffered pin, rather than a derivative.

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### Locality and infinite bounded slabs

<a id="stmt-lem-slab-limits"></a>
<div id="lem:slab-limits" class="lemma">

**Lemma 13** (Local limits on a bounded slab). *On each bounded slab, the finite local functions $h_u,G_u,h_{u,i},h_{u,i}',R_{u,i}$, the probability and residue measures, and the data $m_u,\ell_u,\ell_{u,i}'$ have unique limits as finite graph balls increase to a component. They are measurable and independent of the exhaustion, and the local Cauchy-transform and Poisson-transform identities pass to the limits. Their supports have a common fixed-slab bound, and Equation <a href="#slab:local-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="slab:local-bounds">[slab:local-bounds]</a> continues to hold.*

*The Laurent coefficients through order $k$ of $h_u/z$, $zG_u$, $h_{u,i}/z$, $h_{u,i}'/z$, and $R_{u,i}$, and the moments of $\gamma_u$ through order $k$, depend only on the data within graph distance $k$ of $u$. The same assertion holds for coefficients of logarithmic derivatives under data perturbations, with the local perturbation directions included in the data. It remains valid under arbitrary deletion configurations.*

*At an unassigned vertex the buffered pin inequality of Lemma <a href="#lem:buffered-pin" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:buffered-pin">11</a> holds for these infinite local data. If every base vertex is unassigned, then $m_u=0$ on every bounded slab.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-slab-limits"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-slab-limits</summary>

*Proof.* We first establish locality directly at the level of formal Laurent series, so that the assertion does not depend on any limiting argument. For a finite principal set $Q$ and $v\in Q$, put $H_{v,Q}=h_{v,Q}/z$. Expanding the determinant expectation according to the singleton or nontrivial permutation cycle containing $v$ gives <a id="slab:local-expansion"></a>$$H_{v,Q}=1+\frac{a_v}{z}
 -\sum_{\substack{C\ni v\\ |C|\geq2}}
 w_Cz^{-|C|}
 \prod_{x\in C\setminus\{v\}}H_{x,Q(x)}^{-1}.
 \label{slab:local-expansion}$$ Here $w_C$ is the cycle weight in Equation <a href="#poly:cycle-weight" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="poly:cycle-weight">[poly:cycle-weight]</a>. Delete $v$ first and then the other vertices of $C$ in a fixed order; $Q(x)$ denotes the principal set remaining just before $x$ is deleted. The inverse quotients in the product telescope to $f_{Q\setminus C}/f_{Q\setminus\{v\}}$ after the displayed powers of $z$ are accounted for. Each oriented permutation cycle is counted once, with the convention of the finite expansion.

All series $H$ have constant term $1$. A coefficient of order at most $k$ in a term coming from a cycle of length $l$ uses only coefficients through order $k-l$ in its inverse factors. Induction on $k$ now proves the radius-$k$ locality assertion: the cycle has length at most $k$, and the further radii of the needed factors stay within distance $k$ of $v$. Formal inversion, multiplication, and differentiation preserve this property. This proves locality for all the listed series and for their perturbation derivatives, including complex perturbations; only the algebraic finite polynomial identities were used. The expansion of $zG_v$ is the moment expansion of $\gamma_v$, proving the moment assertion.

For real data, the measures $\gamma_v$ are probability measures with support in the common compact interval $[-B,B]$. The residue measures have support there and bounded mass for each fixed vertex, uniformly in the finite principal subset. Locality stabilizes all their moments: for the residue measure use the Laurent expansion of $h_v(z)=z+a_v-\int(z-x)^{-1}\,d\nu_v(x)$. Compact-support moment determinacy proves unique weak convergence. It also gives locally uniform convergence of $h_v,G_v$ and their pin alternatives on $\mathbb C\setminus[-B,B]$, in particular on the upper half-plane. Their limits satisfy $\operatorname{Im}h_v(z)\geq\operatorname{Im}z$.

For the finite spectral-shift functions $\xi_h$, we have $0\leq\xi_h\leq1$. Every weak-\* subsequential limit therefore has these bounds. The identity $$\pi^{-1}\arg h(x+i\eta)=(P_\eta*\xi_h)(x)$$ passes to the limit, since the Poisson kernel belongs to $L^1(\mathbb R)$ and the analytic functions converge. Poisson approximation uniquely determines the limit function from these identities. Thus the entire sequence converges weak-\*; all functions vanish above $B$, so their integrals over the positive axis converge. The integrals defining $m$ also converge by the compact support of $\gamma$. Apply this reasoning to every pin alternative. The inequalities in Equation <a href="#slab:local-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="slab:local-bounds">[slab:local-bounds]</a> and Lemma <a href="#lem:buffered-pin" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:buffered-pin">11</a> pass to the limit, the latter because there are only finitely many choices of color.

Finite graph balls and the corresponding finite polynomial computations are measurable in the countable Borel relation. Their limits are therefore measurable. The same construction applies to single-vertex response functions indexed by pairs in an orbit. Components disconnected in the distance graph do not affect these local functions, so they can be treated separately. Throughout this construction the heights and weights are absolute on the lifted orbit; they are not reset when the root changes.

Finally suppose all vertices are unassigned. On a finite set $Q$, the weighted polynomial at common argument $z$ is $\prod_{v\in Q}d_v$ times the unweighted mixed polynomial for $T$, with zero penalties, evaluated at the tuple $(b+z/d_v)_{v\in Q}$. Its multiaffine Taylor coefficients at the constant tuple $b$ are the corresponding smaller principal polynomials evaluated at $b$. All are strictly positive by Equation <a href="#slab:r-choice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="slab:r-choice">[slab:r-choice]</a> and Lemma <a href="#lem:mss-paving" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:mss-paving">8</a>, including the empty polynomial $1$. Hence the weighted polynomial is strictly positive for every real $z\geq0$. All its roots are real, so its local spectral measures have no positive support. Taking the local limits gives $m_u=0$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
#### Uniform comparison under far deletions

<a id="slab:normalized-data"></a>
At an unassigned vertex of a slab define the normalized tuple $$\mathcal D^I(v,s)=e^{-s}
 \bigl(m_{(v,s)},\ell_{(v,s)},
       (\ell_{(v,s),i}')_{1\leq i\leq r}\bigr).
 \label{slab:normalized-data}$$ We use the sum of absolute values as its norm. The superscript records the slab, and will be omitted when the ambient configuration is clear.

<a id="stmt-lem-far-deletion"></a>
<div id="lem:far-deletion" class="lemma">

**Lemma 14** (Far-deletion comparison). *Consider any bounded slab containing $V\times[0,1]$, any base partial assignment, and any further measurable restriction that retains the central vertices being tested. Deleting a measurable set of vertices at heights $s>R$, or at heights $s<-R$, changes the tuple in Equation <a href="#slab:normalized-data" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="slab:normalized-data">[slab:normalized-data]</a> by an amount tending to zero in $L^1(d\mu\,ds)$ on the central unassigned band as $R\to\infty$. The convergence is uniform in the slab, the partial assignment, and the deletion set. The same conclusion holds for the union of a far high set and a far low set.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-far-deletion"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-far-deletion</summary>

*Proof.* We prove the analytic comparisons first and then recover the integral data. The transform $G$ determines the local probability measure; $h=1/G$ and $R_i=h_i/h$ then determine the parent and pinned spectral shifts. For high deletions we test their integrated differences against bounded complex directions and express a single deletion response as a logarithmic derivative. Stability bounds that derivative independently of height. We transport its local Laurent coefficients and use analytic uniqueness to recover the whole-deletion response. Low deletions instead cut a matrix of small norm. All pre- and post-deletion systems use the same absolute heights, penalties, and retained probabilities.

<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
###### High deletions and test directions.

<a id="slab:high-G"></a>
<a id="slab:high-R"></a>
Write $D$ for a set above height $R$, with $R>1$. Then $\widetilde\mu(D)\leq e^{-R}$. For upper-half-plane arguments $z$ we claim $$\begin{aligned}
 \int_{\mathrm{central}}
 \left|G_v^{\mathrm{pre}}(z)-G_v^{\mathrm{post}}(z)\right|
 \,d\widetilde\mu(v)
 &\leq C(z)\widetilde\mu(D), \label{slab:high-G}\\
 \int_{\substack{\mathrm{central}\\\mathrm{unassigned}}}
 \left|R_{v,i}^{\mathrm{pre}}(z)-R_{v,i}^{\mathrm{post}}(z)\right|
 \,d\widetilde\mu(v)
 &\leq C(z)\widetilde\mu(D). \label{slab:high-R}
\end{aligned}$$ Here $v$ denotes a lifted vertex, and $R_{v,i}=h_{v,i}/h_v$ uses the pin with unchanged penalty. The constants are bounded uniformly for $z$ in upper-half-plane compact sets, independently of the slab and assignment.

It suffices to bound the integral of each complex difference against an arbitrary measurable direction $\lambda(v)$ with $\left|\lambda(v)\right|\leq1$, supported on the tested set. For the first comparison perturb $Y_v$ by $\zeta\lambda(v)$. For the second, fix a color $i$ and add $\zeta\lambda(v)$ to its probability at every tested unassigned vertex, leaving the other probabilities unchanged. For this second perturbation, first use unnormalized weights in the polynomial sum. Equivalently, normalize the weights at each vertex and multiply the resulting polynomial on $Q$ by $\prod_{v\in Q}(1+\zeta\lambda(v))$. These additional factors cancel in the quotient deleting one high vertex $u$, since $\lambda(u)=0$.

<a id="slab:derivative-identity"></a>
In a finite principal system, differentiation of the numerator and denominator yields $$U_u(z):=\left.\partial_\zeta\log h_u(z;\zeta)\right|_{\zeta=0}
 =\sum_{v\ \mathrm{tested}}\lambda(v)
   \bigl(\mathcal B_v^{\mathrm{pre}}(z)
        -\mathcal B_v^{\mathrm{post}(u)}(z)\bigr),
 \label{slab:derivative-identity}$$ where $\mathcal B_v=G_v$ or $R_{v,i}$, respectively, and $\mathrm{post}(u)$ means deletion of this one vertex. For the probability perturbation, the unnormalized derivative at $v$ is exactly the polynomial with $v$ pinned to color $i$; its logarithmic derivative is $R_{v,i}$. The cancellation just noted allows us to estimate $h_u(z;\zeta)$ using the normalized determinant formula in Lemma <a href="#lem:stability" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:stability">6</a>.

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
###### A uniform bound for the logarithmic derivative.

Fix $\eta>0$ and upper arguments with $\operatorname{Im}z\geq\eta$. There is a disk $\left|\zeta\right|<s_*$, independent of the slab, directions, finite principal set, and assignment, on which $\operatorname{Im}h_u(z;\zeta)\geq\eta/2$. For the diagonal perturbation, the relevant determinant matrices change by norm at most $\left|\zeta\right|$. For the probability perturbation, all tested vertices initially have probabilities $1/r$. The normalized probabilities have analytic square roots near these positive values on a common disk $\left|\zeta\right|<c/r$, and their changes are bounded by a constant times $\left|\zeta\right|$ on a smaller disk. Square roots at all other vertices are unchanged, including the zero probabilities at assigned vertices. Every changed matrix entry is incident to the central band, and its other endpoint has height in $[-\log J,1+\log J]$ by Equation <a href="#slab:edge-cocycle" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="slab:edge-cocycle">[slab:edge-cocycle]</a>. Bounded row and column sums, using the degree bound, therefore bound the entire matrix perturbation by $C\left|\zeta\right|$, uniformly in the enclosing slab.

The initial determinant matrices are Hermitian. Taking $s_*$ small enough, their perturbed numerical ranges have imaginary parts less than $\eta/2$ in absolute value. Lemma <a href="#lem:stability" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:stability">6</a> gives nonvanishing when all diagonal variables have imaginary part greater than $\eta/2$, for every principal subset. In the diagonal-perturbation case, regard the shifts as perturbations of the penalties. The final quotient at the unperturbed vertex $u$ is monic affine in $Y_u$; its zero therefore has imaginary part at most $\eta/2$. Evaluating at the common argument $z$ proves the asserted lower bound on the imaginary part of $h_u(z;\zeta)$.

<a id="slab:uniform-U"></a>
A holomorphic logarithm on the $\zeta$ disk now has imaginary part between $0$ and $\pi$. The harmonic gradient estimate and the Cauchy–Riemann equations give $$\left|U_u(z)\right|\leq C(\eta).
 \label{slab:uniform-U}$$ The bound does not involve the size of $h_u$ or the height of $u$. Thus high vertices with arbitrarily large real weights cause no loss of uniformity.

For each fixed bounded enclosing slab, we also need uniform control near infinity. On a common small $\zeta$ disk all perturbed determinant matrices then have a uniform norm bound. Choose $B'$ larger than this bound and larger than every real support bound, with positive slack. For $\left|z\right|>B'$, use the half-plane in the direction $z/\left|z\right|$ strictly beyond the matrix norm bound. The affine quotient belongs to a rotated half-plane through the origin, so the same logarithmic argument gives a uniform bound for $U_u$ there. This bound and $B'$ may depend on the fixed slab; they are uniform in its principal subsets, directions, and assignment states. In particular the functions $U_u$ are analytic and locally bounded on <a id="slab:normal-domain"></a>$$\Omega=\mathbb C^+\ \cup\ \{z:\left|z\right|>B'\},
 \label{slab:normal-domain}$$ and are bounded near the removable point at infinity.

As finite balls around $u$ increase, normal families give subsequential locally uniform limits on $\Omega$, also near infinity. The locality in Lemma <a href="#lem:slab-limits" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:slab-limits">13</a>, applied to perturbation derivatives, stabilizes every Laurent coefficient at infinity. All subsequential limits therefore have the same Laurent series and coincide by the identity theorem on the connected domain $\Omega$. This defines a unique measurable function $U_u$ for infinite components, retaining Equation <a href="#slab:uniform-U" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="slab:uniform-U">[slab:uniform-U]</a>.

The endpoint functions $\mathcal B_v$ are also analytic on $\Omega$, with removable values at infinity. At central vertices they are uniformly bounded on upper-half-plane compact sets. Indeed the residue representation gives $$\left|h_{v,i}(z)\right|\leq\left|z\right|+a_v+
       \frac{\sum_j\nu_{v,i,j}}{\operatorname{Im}z},
 \qquad \operatorname{Im}h_v(z)\geq\operatorname{Im}z,$$ and the central residue masses and penalties are uniformly bounded. The same statements hold after deletions.

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
###### Recovering the whole-deletion response.

Let $H_*(z)$ be the integral against $\lambda(v)$ of the difference between the endpoint functions before and after deleting all of $D$. The preceding bounds show that $H_*$ is analytic on $\Omega$, including at infinity. We do not sum individual analytic responses over an entire orbit. Instead we first match their local Laurent coefficients.

For each integer $k\geq1$, partition $D$ measurably into finitely many batches such that distinct vertices of a batch have distance greater than $2k$ in the enclosing graph, and delete the batches in order. The required finite Borel coloring exists for every bounded-degree Borel graph and each finite-distance graph power <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[KST99](#ref-KST99)]. For completeness, choose a countable family of Borel binary labels separating points; assign each vertex a finite prefix long enough to distinguish it from its finitely many neighbors. These prefixes give a countable proper Borel coloring. Process the countably many color classes in order, recoloring each class with a palette of size one more than the degree bound and avoiding the previously assigned neighbor colors. Each class is independent, so this is a proper finite Borel coloring.

For each batch use the single-deletion logarithmic derivative in the configuration just before that batch, and put $$S_k(z)=\sum_{\mathrm{batches}}\int_{\mathrm{batch}}
 U_u^{\mathrm{pre}}(z)\,d\widetilde\mu(u).$$ Because the batches partition $D$, Equation <a href="#slab:uniform-U" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="slab:uniform-U">[slab:uniform-U]</a> gives the locally uniform bound $\left|S_k(z)\right|\leq C(z)\widetilde\mu(D)$ on $\mathbb C^+$, independent of $k$ and of the number of batches. There is a common fixed-slab bound near infinity as well.

The Laurent coefficients of $S_k$ and $H_*$ agree through order $k$. To see this, first apply Equation <a href="#slab:derivative-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="slab:derivative-identity">[slab:derivative-identity]</a> to one deletion. At these orders only vertices within distance $k$ of the deletion can contribute, by Lemma <a href="#lem:slab-limits" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:slab-limits">13</a>; hence this coefficient identity on an infinite component is verified in a sufficiently large finite neighborhood. Within one batch the coefficient change at a tested vertex is the sum of the single changes in the pre-batch configuration, because at most one batch vertex belongs to its radius-$k$ neighborhood. Sum this coefficient identity over the tested vertices and use Equation <a href="#slab:mass-transport" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="slab:mass-transport">[slab:mass-transport]</a> to move the sum to the deletion vertices. The transports are integrable: for the fixed slab and fixed order their coefficients are bounded, and their supports have a uniform finite ball-size bound. The analytic bounds near infinity justify interchanging coefficients and integrals. Finally sum over the batches; the successive endpoint changes telescope to $H_*$.

Apply normal families to $S_k$ on $\Omega$ with its removable point at infinity. Every subsequential limit has every Laurent coefficient of $H_*$, since the first $k$ coefficients agree for each $k$. The identity theorem shows that this limit is $H_*$ throughout $\Omega$. It follows that $\left|H_*(z)\right|\leq C(z)\widetilde\mu(D)$ on $\mathbb C^+$, with the slab-independent constants already established there. The fixed-slab compactness near infinity has thus transferred the uniform upper-half-plane bound; it has not supplied a spectral-support bound uniform over slabs. For each fixed target $z$, choosing the measurable complex sign $\lambda$ of the endpoint difference proves Equations <a href="#slab:high-G" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="slab:high-G">[slab:high-G]</a> and <a href="#slab:high-R" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="slab:high-R">[slab:high-R]</a>.

At central vertices $h_v$ and $h_{v,i}$ are bounded uniformly on upper-half-plane compact sets. The identity $h^{\mathrm{pre}}-h^{\mathrm{post}}
=h^{\mathrm{pre}}h^{\mathrm{post}}(G^{\mathrm{post}}-G^{\mathrm{pre}})$ therefore gives the same $L^1$ comparison for $h$. Then $h_i=hR_i$ gives the comparison for every $h_i$ on the central unassigned set. Adding the unchanged shift $(K-1)a_v$ gives it for $h_i'$ as well.

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
###### Low deletions.

Suppose now that $D$ lies below height $-R$. Cut every edge between $D$ and its complement, writing $V_0$ for the removed matrix. Each such edge has both heights below $-R+\log J$. Its entry is therefore $O(e^{-R})$; the degree bound yields $\left\lVert V_0\right\rVert=O(e^{-R})$ uniformly in the slab. Interpolate by $W(t)=W_{\mathrm{cut}}+tV_0$, $0\leq t\leq1$. For finite systems at a central vertex, $h_v(z;t)$ lies in the upper half-plane and is bounded uniformly for upper $z$ in compact sets, by the central second-moment and residue estimates. Around each real $t$, allow complex perturbations in a disk of radius $c\eta/(r\left\lVert V_0\right\rVert)$ when $\left\lVert V_0\right\rVert>0$, where $\operatorname{Im}z\geq\eta$. The corresponding determinant matrix perturbation has norm at most $c\eta$. The complex interpolation still has zero diagonal, so the algebraic identity and conditional half-plane assertion of Lemma <a href="#lem:stability" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:stability">6</a> apply even though the matrix need not be Hermitian away from real $t$. The stability and logarithmic gradient argument just given implies $$\left|\partial_t\log h_v(z;t)\right|
 \leq C(\eta,r)\left\lVert V_0\right\rVert.$$ The zero-norm case is immediate. The argument applies also to each prospective buffered pin, considered as a separate real-data system under the same edge interpolation. Integrate in real $t$, using the uniform absolute bound on $h$ and $\operatorname{Im}h\geq\eta$ for reciprocation. We obtain $O(e^{-R})$ endpoint comparisons for $h,G,h_i'$ at every tested central vertex. In the cut system the low component factors out of the polynomial quotients, so the endpoint at $t=0$ has exactly the same central data as the deleted system. The finite-ball limits pass these comparisons to infinite bounded-slab components.

<a id="pm-h-19"></a>
###### Recovering the integral data.

At central vertices the probability measures $\gamma_v$ have uniformly bounded second moments. Therefore $m_v=\int x_+\,d\gamma_v$ is uniformly approximated by integration against continuous compactly supported cutoffs of $x_+$. For such a test function, Poisson convolution approximates it uniformly, so its integral against $\gamma_v$ is in turn uniformly approximated by its integral against $-\pi^{-1}\operatorname{Im}G_v(x+i\eta)\,dx$ at a sufficiently small fixed $\eta>0$.

<a id="slab:xi-tail"></a>
For the $\ell$ data we also have a uniform positive-tail estimate: $$\int_M^\infty\xi_h(x)\,dx
 \leq \frac{\sum_j\nu_j}{M},\qquad M>0.
 \label{slab:xi-tail}$$ For finite systems, $\xi_h(x)=1$ with $x>M$ implies $\sum_j\nu_j/(x-s_j)>x+a_v>M$. The Boole length identity used in Lemma <a href="#lem:buffered-pin" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:buffered-pin">11</a> proves Equation <a href="#slab:xi-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="slab:xi-tail">[slab:xi-tail]</a>; weak-\* convergence on a fixed bounded slab gives the infinite version. The bound is uniform at central vertices and for their pins. After choosing $M$ large, approximate the remaining integral over $(0,M)$ by $$\frac1\pi\int_0^M\arg h(x+i\eta)\,dx.$$ By the Poisson identity, the error is at most $\left\lVert P_\eta*1_{(0,M)}-1_{(0,M)}\right\rVert_{L^1(\mathbb R)}$, which tends to zero as $\eta\downarrow0$. At this fixed height, arguments are Lipschitz-controlled by differences of $h$ values in $\operatorname{Im}h\geq\eta$.

Choose the tail and smoothing errors first; then use the preceding analytic comparisons on the resulting compact upper-half-plane sets and integrate in $x$. This proves convergence for $m,\ell$ and all the $\ell_i'$. On the central band the measures $d\widetilde\mu$ and $d\mu\,ds$, and the normalization factors $e^{-s}$, are boundedly comparable, proving the claimed tuple comparison. A union of far high and far low deletions is handled in two successive steps. Non-strict far cutoffs have the same conclusion, by inserting a unit of slack in $R$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-20"></a>
#### A color choice independent of height

Figure <a href="#fig:maharam" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:maharam">1</a> summarizes how the far-deletion estimates allow a single base color to be used throughout a long slab.

<a id="stmt-lem-coherent-choice"></a>
<div id="lem:coherent-choice" class="lemma">

<a id="slab:coherent-inequality"></a>
**Lemma 15** (Coherent choices). *For every $\tau_0>0$ there are arbitrarily large integers $L\geq1$ with the following property, uniformly over all base partial assignments. On the slab $V\times[0,L]$ one can choose a measurable prospective color $i(v)$ at each unassigned base vertex, using the same color at every height, so that $$\frac1L\int_{\mathrm{unassigned}\ \mathrm{in}\ V\times[0,L]}
       (\ell_{(v,s),i(v)}'-\ell_{(v,s)})\,d\widetilde\mu(v,s)
 \leq C\Phi+\tau_0,
 \qquad
 \Phi=\frac1L\int_{V\times[0,L]}m_{(v,s)}\,d\widetilde\mu(v,s).
 \label{slab:coherent-inequality}$$ Here $C$ is the universal constant in Lemma <a href="#lem:buffered-pin" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:buffered-pin">11</a>, and every $\ell_{u,i}'$ on the left is the insertion cost after changing only the lifted vertex $u$. The same $L$ can be used at every stage of a sequence of partial assignments.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-coherent-choice"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-coherent-choice</summary>

<a id="slab:translation"></a>
*Proof.* The normalized tuple obeys the exact translation rule $$\mathcal D^I(v,s+\alpha)=\mathcal D^{I-\alpha}(v,s).
 \label{slab:translation}$$ Indeed translating the heights by $\alpha$ multiplies all weights $d$, the matrix $W$, and the penalties in the orbit by $e^\alpha$. The spectral quantities $m,\ell,\ell_i'$ scale by this same factor, which is canceled by the normalization at the root. The lifted probabilities are unchanged because the partial assignment is on the base. This proves Equation <a href="#slab:translation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="slab:translation">[slab:translation]</a> first in finite balls and then in the local limits.

<a id="slab:reference-slice"></a>
Take a large integer $R$, use the reference interval $[-R,R]$, and write its tuple as $\mathcal D^0$ in this proof. For $\alpha\in[-1,1]$, compare this interval and its translate $[-R,R]-\alpha$ to their intersection. The differences involve only far high and low deletions. Equations <a href="#slab:translation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="slab:translation">[slab:translation]</a> and Lemma <a href="#lem:far-deletion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:far-deletion">14</a> imply <a id="slab:small-translation"></a>$$\int_{v\ \mathrm{unassigned}}
 \int_{\{s\in[0,1]:\ s+\alpha\in[0,1]\}}
 \left|\mathcal D^0(v,s+\alpha)-\mathcal D^0(v,s)\right|
 \,ds\,d\mu(v)\leq e(R),
 \label{slab:small-translation}$$ where $e(R)\to0$ uniformly in the assignment. We enlarge $e(R)$ if necessary so that it is positive. Integrating in $\alpha$ turns the left side into the corresponding integral over all pairs $(s,t)\in[0,1]^2$. Fubini therefore gives a single $s_0\in[0,1]$ such that $$\int_{v\ \mathrm{unassigned}}\int_0^1
 \left|\mathcal D^0(v,s)-\mathcal D^0(v,s_0)\right|
 \,ds\,d\mu(v)\leq3e(R).
 \label{slab:reference-slice}$$ The slice can also be chosen so that the local data and the buffered pin inequality hold almost everywhere on it.

At this slice choose the least minimizing color for $\ell_{(v,s_0),i}'$. This is a measurable function $i(v)$ because there are finitely many colors. Lemma <a href="#lem:buffered-pin" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:buffered-pin">11</a> gives $$e^{-s_0}\bigl(\ell_{(v,s_0),i(v)}'-\ell_{(v,s_0)}
                         -Cm_{(v,s_0)}\bigr)\leq0.$$ The tuple norm contains all color alternatives. Consequently Equation <a href="#slab:reference-slice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="slab:reference-slice">[slab:reference-slice]</a> implies that the integral of this same normalized difference over $v$ and $s\in[0,1]$, now using the data at $s$, is at most a fixed constant times $e(R)$.

Consider a unit band $[k,k+1]$ in $[0,L]$, where $k$ is an integer with $k\geq R$ and $L-k\geq R$. Translate it to $[0,1]$; its ambient interval becomes $[-k,L-k]$. Trimming this interval to $[-R,R]$ removes only far high and low vertices. Lemma <a href="#lem:far-deletion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:far-deletion">14</a> and Equation <a href="#slab:translation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="slab:translation">[slab:translation]</a> show that its tuple differs from $\mathcal D^0(v,s)$ in integrated norm by at most $e(R)$, after enlarging the same uniformly vanishing error function. Thus the $\mu\,ds$ integral over this unit band of $$e^{-s}\bigl(\ell_{(v,s),i(v)}'-\ell_{(v,s)}-Cm_{(v,s)}\bigr)$$ on unassigned vertices is at most a constant times $e(R)$. All remaining boundary bands have total length at most $2R+2$. Their normalized data are uniformly bounded by Equation <a href="#slab:local-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="slab:local-bounds">[slab:local-bounds]</a>.

Absolute costs against $d\widetilde\mu$ are exactly normalized costs against $d\mu\,ds$. Summing the unit-band estimates and dividing by $L$ bounds the total error by $C_1e(R)+C_2(R+1)/L$, with constants independent of the assignment. Choose $R$ sufficiently large, then choose $L$ sufficiently large and satisfying any prescribed lower bound. This makes the error at most $\tau_0$. Finally $m\geq0$ permits replacing its integral over unassigned vertices by the integral over the whole slab, yielding Equation <a href="#slab:coherent-inequality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="slab:coherent-inequality">[slab:coherent-inequality]</a>. All choices of the numerical parameters used only uniform estimates, proving the assertion about using the same $L$ at different stages. Null modifications of base data can be absorbed into an invariant null set by nonsingularity. ◻

</details>

<figure id="fig:maharam">

<figcaption>Height geometry behind Lemmas <a href="#lem:far-deletion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:far-deletion">14</a> and <a href="#lem:coherent-choice" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:coherent-choice">15</a> (not to scale). High and low deletions are controlled by different estimates. A color selected on the reference slice is used at every height above the same base vertex. Translated interior unit bands compare with the reference slab in integrated normalized data; the boundary bands have total length at most $2R + 2$. The colored vertical lines are base fibers ${v} × [0,L]$, not lifted relation orbits.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="sec:pinning"></a>
### Sparse pinning and descent

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
#### Sparse pinning and removal of unassigned vertices

We retain the notation and hypotheses of Theorem <a href="#thm:measured-paving" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:measured-paving">12</a>. In particular, $b>0$ and $r$ satisfy its strict largest-root inequality, and $K,C$ are the universal constants from Lemma <a href="#lem:buffered-pin" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:buffered-pin">11</a>. The lifted graph on a slab is always the restriction of the original enclosing graph, including edges at which a particular matrix entry happens to vanish. All distances below refer to this enclosing graph.

<a id="stmt-prop-sparse-pinning"></a>
<div id="prop:sparse-pinning" class="proposition">

<a id="pin:surviving-operator"></a>
<a id="pin:surviving-cost"></a>
**Proposition 16**. *For every $\zeta,\eta>0$ and $L_0\geq1$, there are an integer $L\geq L_0$ and a measurable partition $$V=\mathcal U\sqcup V_1\sqcup\cdots\sqcup V_r$$ such that $\mu(\mathcal U)<\eta$ and the following holds. On the surviving lifted vertices $(V\setminus\mathcal U)\times[0,L]$, let $T_{\mathrm{col}}^L$ be the restriction of $T$, keeping only entries between vertices whose basepoints belong to the same $V_i$, and put $$\label{pin:surviving-operator}
 A_L=d^{1/2}(T_{\mathrm{col}}^L-Kb\mathbf 1)d^{1/2},
 \qquad d_{(v,s)}=e^s.$$ Extend $m_u^{\mathrm{surv}}=((A_L)_+)_{uu}$ by zero on the deleted vertices. Then $$\label{pin:surviving-cost}
 \frac1L\int_{V\times[0,L]}m_u^{\mathrm{surv}}\,
 d\widetilde\mu(u)<\zeta.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-sparse-pinning"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-sparse-pinning</summary>

<a id="pin:polynomial-approximation"></a>
<a id="pin:cost-approximation"></a>
*Proof.* We first construct a partial assignment with small mixed-polynomial cost. Polynomial moments depend on finite graph balls, so sufficiently sparse simultaneous pins have an additive first-order response except on an event of quadratic order in the update probability. Approximating the derivative of the positive-part function controls the error in that response independently of the number of roots. Parameters used to select the slab will be specified below. On a fixed slab $[0,L]$, write $$\Phi=\frac1L\int_{V\times[0,L]}m_u\,d\widetilde\mu(u).$$ Choose a common spectral support bound $[-B,B]$ for all the finite principal systems, pin alternatives, and deletion configurations on this slab, as in Lemma <a href="#lem:slab-limits" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:slab-limits">13</a>. For $\tau>0$, choose a real polynomial $P$ such that $$\label{pin:polynomial-approximation}
 P(-B)=0,\qquad
 \int_{-B}^B\left|P'(x)-\mathbf 1_{(0,\infty)}(x)\right|\,dx\leq\tau.$$ For example, approximate the indicated step function first by a continuous function in $L^1([-B,B])$, then uniformly by a polynomial, and integrate that polynomial from $-B$. It follows that $$\sup_{[-B,B]}\left|P(x)-x_+\right|\leq\tau.$$ Define $$m_{P,u}=\int P(x)\,d\gamma_u(x),\qquad
 \Phi_P=\frac1L\int_{V\times[0,L]}m_{P,u}\,d\widetilde\mu(u).$$ Since $\gamma_u$ is a probability measure and $\widetilde\mu(V\times[0,L])=1-e^{-L}\leq1$, we have $$\label{pin:cost-approximation}
 \left|\Phi-\Phi_P\right|\leq\tau \qquad(L\geq1).$$

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
###### The response to one buffered pin.

<a id="pin:single-response"></a>
At an unassigned lifted vertex $u$, consider pinning to a specified color $i$, increasing its penalty from $bd_u$ to $Kbd_u$ at the same time. The total polynomial response on its component is $$\label{pin:single-response}
 \begin{split}
 D_P(u)&=\sum_y\bigl(m_{P,y}^{\mathrm{pin}\ u\mathrm{\ to\ }i}
                         -m_{P,y}^{\mathrm{pre}}\bigr)\\
 &=\int_{-B}^B P'(x)
       \bigl(\xi_{h_{u,i}'}(x)-\xi_{h_u}(x)\bigr)\,dx.
 \end{split}$$ Here $h_{u,i}'$ denotes the buffered alternative; the prime does not denote differentiation. To prove the identity on a finite principal set, use the fact that the sum of its local spectral probability measures is the counting measure of the roots. The pre-pin and post-pin polynomials have equal degree and the same principal polynomial with $u$ removed. Integrating the difference of their root counts above $x$ against $P'(x)$ gives exactly <a href="#pin:single-response" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pin:single-response">[pin:single-response]</a>.

<a id="pin:single-error"></a>
Put $k=\max\{1,\deg P\}$.
