By the locality in Lemma <a href="#lem:slab-limits" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:slab-limits">13</a>, only vertices at distance at most $k$ from $u$ contribute to the sum on the first line of <a href="#pin:single-response" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pin:single-response">[pin:single-response]</a>. Compute in finite balls large enough to contain these vertices and their relevant neighborhoods. The local polynomial moments stabilize, while the functions $\xi$ converge weak-\* on $[-B,B]$. This proves the identity on infinite components as well. Since both $\xi$-functions take values in $[0,1]$ almost everywhere, <a href="#pin:polynomial-approximation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pin:polynomial-approximation">[pin:polynomial-approximation]</a> implies $$\label{pin:single-error}
 \left|D_P(u)-(\ell_{u,i}'-\ell_u)\right|\leq\tau.$$

<a id="pm-h-24"></a>
###### One sparse update.

For a current partial base assignment, choose the prospective colors $i(v)$ supplied by Lemma <a href="#lem:coherent-choice" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:coherent-choice">15</a>, with tolerance $\tau_0>0$. The color $i(v)$ is the same at all heights over $v$. Properly color the distance-$2k$ power of the base graph with finitely many auxiliary Borel color classes. The finite Borel coloring fact used in the proof of Lemma <a href="#lem:far-deletion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:far-deletion">14</a> applies because this power still has bounded degree. Independently for these auxiliary classes, select a Bernoulli-$\alpha$ indicator, where $0<\alpha<1$. Every currently unassigned base vertex in a selected class is pinned to its prospective color, with its penalty buffered, at all heights simultaneously.

Within a radius-$k$ lifted ball, different vertices project to different base vertices, and their pairwise base distance is at most $2k$. They consequently have different auxiliary classes. The indicators of all candidate pins affecting the local moment $m_{P,y}$ are therefore independent. This assertion uses injectivity of projection within each lifted orbit; it does not require independent indicators at all vertices of the base.

<a id="pin:sparse-update"></a>
If at most one candidate in this local ball is selected, the change in $m_{P,y}$ is exactly the sum of its selected single-pin responses. A ball has at most a fixed finite number $N$ of vertices, so the probability of two or more selections is at most $\binom{N}{2}\alpha^2$. On this event the discrepancy between the actual change and the sum of single-pin responses is bounded in absolute value by a constant depending only on $N$ and $\sup_{[-B,B]}\left|P\right|$. Integrate this estimate and exchange roots and pin locations using <a href="#slab:mass-transport" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="slab:mass-transport">[slab:mass-transport]</a>. The result is $$\label{pin:sparse-update}
 \mathbb E[\Phi_P^{\mathrm{new}}\mid\mathrm{pre}]
 \leq\Phi_P+
   \frac\alpha L\int_{\mathrm{unassigned}}D_P(u)\,
                  d\widetilde\mu(u)+C_P\alpha^2.$$ All the transports here are measurable, uniformly bounded on the fixed slab, and supported on bounded-radius pairs. Thus they are integrable and the signed version of mass transport is applicable. The constant $C_P$ may depend on the slab, graph and polynomial, but not on the current assignment, the prospective choices, or $\alpha$.

<a id="pin:recurrence"></a>
By Lemma <a href="#lem:coherent-choice" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:coherent-choice">15</a>, <a href="#pin:single-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pin:single-error">[pin:single-error]</a>, and <a href="#pin:cost-approximation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pin:cost-approximation">[pin:cost-approximation]</a>, <a href="#pin:sparse-update" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pin:sparse-update">[pin:sparse-update]</a> gives $$\label{pin:recurrence}
 \mathbb E[\Phi_P^{\mathrm{new}}\mid\mathrm{pre}]
 \leq(1+C\alpha)\Phi_P+
       \alpha\bigl(\tau_0+(1+C)\tau+C_P\alpha\bigr).$$ The same $L$ from Lemma <a href="#lem:coherent-choice" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:coherent-choice">15</a> works for all partial base assignments, so this step can be repeated.

<a id="pm-h-25"></a>
###### Iteration and choice of parameters.

Start with every vertex unassigned. The initial cost is $\Phi=0$ by Lemma <a href="#lem:slab-limits" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:slab-limits">13</a>. Use fresh independent class indicators at each step and recalculate the prospective colors from the current assignment. Given $S>0$, perform $n=\lceil S/\alpha\rceil$ steps. For these fixed parameters there are only finitely many possible histories. At every node one may choose separately the measurable prospective-color functions furnished by Lemma <a href="#lem:coherent-choice" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:coherent-choice">15</a>; no selection measurable in an infinite-dimensional space of assignments is needed.

<a id="pin:iteration"></a>
We have $n\alpha\leq S+1$ and $(1+C\alpha)^n\leq e^{C(S+1)}$. Iterating <a href="#pin:recurrence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pin:recurrence">[pin:recurrence]</a>, using the initial bound $\Phi_P\leq\tau$, and then using <a href="#pin:cost-approximation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pin:cost-approximation">[pin:cost-approximation]</a> again yields $$\label{pin:iteration}
 \mathbb E\Phi_{\mathrm{final}}
 \leq\tau+e^{C(S+1)}
   \bigl[\tau+(S+1)(\tau_0+(1+C)\tau+C_P\alpha)\bigr].$$ If $\mathcal U$ is the final unassigned base set, each still-unassigned vertex has conditional probability $\alpha$ of being selected at the next step, regardless of its prospective color. Therefore <a id="pin:unassigned-measure"></a>$$\label{pin:unassigned-measure}
 \mathbb E\mu(\mathcal U)=(1-\alpha)^n\leq e^{-S}.$$

<a id="pin:chosen-history"></a>
Here is an order of choices which makes both expectations small. First take $S$ so large that $e^{-S}<\eta/4$. Next take $\tau_0,\tau>0$ so small that the terms in <a href="#pin:iteration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pin:iteration">[pin:iteration]</a> not involving $C_P\alpha$ have sum less than $\zeta/8$. Choose an integer $L\geq L_0$ using Lemma <a href="#lem:coherent-choice" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:coherent-choice">15</a> for this $\tau_0$. Now choose $B$ and $P$ as above, thereby fixing $C_P$. Finally take $\alpha$ small enough that the remaining term in <a href="#pin:iteration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pin:iteration">[pin:iteration]</a> is less than $\zeta/8$. Thus $$\mathbb E\Phi_{\mathrm{final}}<\zeta/4,
 \qquad \mathbb E\mu(\mathcal U)<\eta/4.$$ Both random variables are nonnegative. By Markov’s inequality and a union bound, some history satisfies simultaneously $$\label{pin:chosen-history}
 \Phi_{\mathrm{final}}<\zeta,
 \qquad\mu(\mathcal U)<\eta.$$ Fix such a history.

<a id="pm-h-26"></a>
###### Removing the unassigned vertices.

We show that deleting $\mathcal U\times[0,L]$ cannot increase the integrated positive cost. Keep the initial and final deletion states fixed. For an arbitrary $\vartheta>0$, choose another polynomial $Q$ with $$Q(-B)=0,\qquad
 \int_{-B}^B\left|Q'(x)-\mathbf 1_{(0,\infty)}(x)\right|\,dx
 \leq\vartheta.$$ At each intermediate deletion state, extend $m_{Q,y}$ by zero at vertices that have already been deleted.

<a id="pin:single-deletion"></a>
For one deletion at $u$, the total pre-minus-post polynomial cost is $$\label{pin:single-deletion}
 \sum_y\bigl(m_{Q,y}^{\mathrm{pre}}-m_{Q,y}^{\mathrm{post}}\bigr)
     =\int_{-B}^B Q'(x)\xi_{h_u}(x)\,dx\geq-\vartheta.$$ For finite sets the identity again follows by integrating the difference of root counts; the boundary term caused by the loss of one root is $Q(-B)=0$. The inequality uses $0\leq\xi_{h_u}\leq1$. The finite-range locality argument used for <a href="#pin:single-response" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pin:single-response">[pin:single-response]</a> proves the identity in an infinite component.

Partition the set to be deleted into finitely many Borel batches whose distinct vertices have distance greater than $2\max\{1,\deg Q\}$ within each batch in the enclosing lifted graph. For one batch, at most one deletion affects any local polynomial moment, so the batch change is exactly the sum of single-deletion responses in its pre-state. Mass transport, <a href="#pin:single-deletion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pin:single-deletion">[pin:single-deletion]</a>, and telescoping over the batches show that the integrated pre-minus-post polynomial cost, divided by $L$, is at least $$-\vartheta\,
   \frac{\widetilde\mu(\mathcal U\times[0,L])}{L}.$$ The error is charged once to each deleted vertex; it is not multiplied by the number of batches. Intermediate deleted sets need not be constant in the height variable, since Lemma <a href="#lem:slab-limits" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:slab-limits">13</a> allows arbitrary deletion configurations in the enclosing slab.

At each endpoint the integrated error between the $Q$-cost and the positive cost is at most $\vartheta$. Thus the pre-minus-post positive cost is at least $-3\vartheta$. The endpoints are independent of $Q$ and of the batch decomposition, so letting $\vartheta\downarrow0$ proves the desired monotonicity.

All surviving vertices are now deterministically colored, with penalty $Kbd_u$. Their finite mixed polynomial is the characteristic polynomial of the corresponding restriction of <a href="#pin:surviving-operator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pin:surviving-operator">[pin:surviving-operator]</a>, so its local measure is the diagonal spectral probability measure of that operator. On an infinite component the same assertion follows from stabilization of polynomial moments and the common compact spectral support. Hence the surviving positive cost is exactly $m_u^{\mathrm{surv}}$. Monotonicity and <a href="#pin:chosen-history" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pin:chosen-history">[pin:chosen-history]</a> give <a href="#pin:surviving-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pin:surviving-cost">[pin:surviving-cost]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-27"></a>
#### Descent from a slab to the base relation

The next estimate converts the integrated weighted cost into the desired spectral error on the base. A finite ball with a large eigenvalue forces a definite positive cost in its lift; mass transport then bounds the measure of such bad balls. For an integer $k\geq1$, let $N_k$ be a uniform upper bound for the number of vertices in a radius-$k$ ball of the base graph.

<a id="stmt-lem-descent"></a>
<div id="lem:descent" class="lemma">

<a id="pin:descent-bound"></a>
**Lemma 17**. *Fix $\sigma,\beta>0$, and choose a real polynomial $p$ of degree at most $k\geq1$ such that $$\sup_{x\in[-1,1]}
 \left|p(x)-(x-(Kb+\sigma))_+^2\right|\leq\beta.$$ Suppose a base partial assignment, with unassigned set $\mathcal U$, and a slab length $L\geq\max\{1,4k\log J\}$ have surviving cost $$\Psi=\frac1L\int_{V\times[0,L]}m_u^{\mathrm{surv}}\,
                         d\widetilde\mu(u),$$ where $m^{\mathrm{surv}}$ is defined from <a href="#pin:surviving-operator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pin:surviving-operator">[pin:surviving-operator]</a>. Complete the base coloring by giving $\mathcal U$ one additional color, and denote its paved matrix system by $T_{\mathrm{pav}}$. Then $$\label{pin:descent-bound}
 \int_V\bigl((T_{\mathrm{pav}}-(Kb+\sigma)\mathbf 1)_+^2\bigr)_{vv}
                   \,d\mu(v)
 \leq2\beta+\mu(\mathcal U)+\frac{2J^kN_k}{\sigma}\Psi.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-descent"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-descent</summary>

*Proof.* Put $g(x)=(x-(Kb+\sigma))_+^2$. Since the paved operator is a compression by mutually orthogonal color projections, it remains a self-adjoint contraction; in particular $0\leq g(T_{\mathrm{pav}})
\leq\mathbf 1$.

<a id="pin:failure-reduction"></a>
For an assigned base vertex $v$, let $B_k(v)$ be its radius-$k$ ball in the base graph, and let $T_v$ be the finite compression of the paved matrix to the assigned vertices of this ball. Polynomial diagonal moments through degree $k$ at $v$ are unchanged by this compression. Indeed every walk contributing to such a moment stays within $B_k(v)$, and edges from assigned to unassigned vertices are zero after paving. Consequently $$(p(T_{\mathrm{pav}}))_{vv}=(p(T_v))_{vv}.$$ Let $\mathcal F$ be the measurable set of assigned vertices for which $\lambda_{\max}(T_v)>Kb+\sigma$. Measurability follows by finite matrix evaluation on the Borel balls. If $v\notin\mathcal F$ is assigned, then $g(T_v)=0$ and the two polynomial approximation errors give $$(g(T_{\mathrm{pav}}))_{vv}\leq2\beta.$$ On $\mathcal F\cup\mathcal U$ its diagonal is at most $1$. Therefore $$\label{pin:failure-reduction}
 \int_V(g(T_{\mathrm{pav}}))_{vv}\,d\mu(v)
 \leq2\beta+\mu(\mathcal U)+\mu(\mathcal F).$$

<a id="pin:positive-upper-bound"></a>
<a id="pin:failure-trace"></a>
It remains to bound $\mu(\mathcal F)$. Set $I_{\mathrm{int}}=[k\log J,L-k\log J]$. For $u=(v,s)$ with $s\in I_{\mathrm{int}}$, the entire ball $B_k(v)$ lifts into the slab: the height change along each graph edge has absolute value at most $\log J$. Let $Q_u$ project onto the assigned vertices of this lifted ball. With $A_L$ as in <a href="#pin:surviving-operator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pin:surviving-operator">[pin:surviving-operator]</a>, the order inequality $A_L\leq(A_L)_+$ gives, after compression and diagonal congruence, $$\label{pin:positive-upper-bound}
 T_v-Kb\mathbf 1
 \leq d_{\mathrm{ball}}^{-1/2}Q_u(A_L)_+Q_u
                                  d_{\mathrm{ball}}^{-1/2}.$$ The base and lifted finite matrices here are identified up to the allowed diagonal unitary gauge, which does not affect the inequality or any diagonal spectral quantities. The right side is positive. If $v\in\mathcal F$, its norm is greater than $\sigma$ by <a href="#pin:positive-upper-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pin:positive-upper-bound">[pin:positive-upper-bound]</a>, and hence its trace is greater than $\sigma$. Equivalently, $$\label{pin:failure-trace}
 \sum_{y\in B_k(u)\ \mathrm{assigned}}
                \frac{m_y^{\mathrm{surv}}}{d_y}>\sigma.$$ Here $B_k(u)$ denotes the lift of the base ball, which agrees with the radius-$k$ enclosing lifted ball at these interior heights. The comparison uses only $A_L\leq(A_L)_+$; it does not require positive part to commute with compression or diagonal congruence.

Integrate <a href="#pin:failure-trace" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pin:failure-trace">[pin:failure-trace]</a> against $d\mu(v)\,ds/L$ over $\mathcal F\times I_{\mathrm{int}}$, and enlarge the resulting nonnegative sum to all interior roots. Since $d\mu(v)\,ds=d_u\,d\widetilde\mu(u)$, the resulting integrand is a sum of $(d_u/d_y)m_y^{\mathrm{surv}}$. On a radius-$k$ ball, $d_u/d_y\leq J^k$. Exchanging roots and targets by <a href="#slab:mass-transport" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="slab:mass-transport">[slab:mass-transport]</a>, each target $y$ has at most $N_k$ possible roots. Deleted vertices may be retained as locations for the enclosing-ball relation, with $m_y^{\mathrm{surv}}=0$ there. Thus $$\sigma\frac{\left|I_{\mathrm{int}}\right|}L\mu(\mathcal F)
 \leq J^kN_k\Psi.$$ Our lower bound on $L$ ensures $\left|I_{\mathrm{int}}\right|/L\geq1/2$, including the case $J=1$. We conclude that $$\mu(\mathcal F)\leq\frac{2J^kN_k}{\sigma}\Psi.$$ Combining this with <a href="#pin:failure-reduction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pin:failure-reduction">[pin:failure-reduction]</a> proves <a href="#pin:descent-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pin:descent-bound">[pin:descent-bound]</a>. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-descent-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-descent</summary>

*Completion of the proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:measured-paving" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:measured-paving">12</a>.* Fix the prescribed $\sigma,\rho>0$. Choose $\beta>0$ with $2\beta<\rho/4$, and then choose the polynomial and its degree $k\geq1$ in Lemma <a href="#lem:descent" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:descent">17</a>. Thus $N_k$ is fixed before selecting the slab. Take $$\eta=\rho/4,\qquad
 \zeta=\frac{\rho\sigma}{8J^kN_k},\qquad
 L_0=\max\{1,4k\log J\}.$$ Proposition <a href="#prop:sparse-pinning" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:sparse-pinning">16</a> supplies a partial assignment and a slab of length at least $L_0$ with $\mu(\mathcal U)<\eta$ and $\Psi<\zeta$. Give $\mathcal U$ the additional color and apply Lemma <a href="#lem:descent" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:descent">17</a>. Its right side is strictly less than $\rho$, as required.

Only the $r$ assigned colors and one extra color occur in the final partition. The auxiliary graph colors, the slab length, the approximation degrees and the number of updates affect its construction but not this number of final colors. In particular the graph and cocycle bounds do not enter the condition on $r$ in the theorem. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:expected"></a>
### Expected maximal abelian subalgebras

We now combine the measured paving theorem with a separate argument for the part of an operator orthogonal to the normalizer algebra. Throughout this section, $M$ has separable predual, $\varphi$ is a faithful normal state, and $A\subset M$ is a MASA contained in the centralizer $M_\varphi$. We write $E_A$ for the $\varphi$-preserving normal conditional expectation and use the sequence quotient of Lemma <a href="#lem:sequence-quotient" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:sequence-quotient">5</a>. Brackets denote classes in that quotient; a fixed operator also denotes its constant sequence. A partition in the quotient will always be represented by partitions in $A$ with one fixed finite number of members.

<a id="stmt-thm-state-paving"></a>
<div id="thm:state-paving" class="theorem">

<a id="exp:state-paving"></a>
**Theorem 18**. *For every $\gamma>0$ there is an integer $R_{\mathrm{st}}(\gamma)$ with the following property. For every self-adjoint $x\in M$ with $\left\lVert x\right\rVert\leq1$, there are partitions $\mathcal P_j$ of $1$ in $A$, each with $R_{\mathrm{st}}(\gamma)$ members, and self-adjoint $w_j\in M$ such that $$\label{exp:state-paving}
 \left\lVert w_j\right\rVert\leq2,\qquad
 \left\lVert w_j\right\rVert_\varphi\longrightarrow0,\qquad
 \left\lVert C_{\mathcal P_j}(x-w_j)-E_A(x)\right\rVert\leq\gamma.$$ The integer $R_{\mathrm{st}}(\gamma)$ is independent of $M,A,\varphi$, and $x$.*

</div>

<a id="pm-h-29"></a>
#### The normalizer algebra

Let $N$ be the von Neumann algebra generated by the groupoid normalizers of $A$ in $M$. These are the partial isometries $v$ with $v^*v,vv^*\in A$ such that conjugation by $v$ carries $A v^*v$ onto $A vv^*$. The algebra $N$ contains $A$. Since the modular group of $\varphi$ fixes $A$ pointwise, it carries every groupoid normalizer to another with the same supports and the same induced corner isomorphism. Hence $N$ is globally modular invariant. Takesaki’s theorem <a id="pm-cite-29"></a>[[Takesaki72](#ref-Takesaki72)] gives a normal $\varphi$-preserving conditional expectation $E_N:M\to N$; uniqueness of the state-preserving expectation onto $A$ gives <a id="exp:expectation-composition"></a>$$\label{exp:expectation-composition}
 E_A E_N=E_A.$$

The inclusion $A\subset N$ is Cartan. For completeness, groupoid regularity here implies regularity by unitary normalizers. Represent $A$ on a standard probability space. A normal isomorphism between two corners of $A$ is represented by a nonsingular measurable partial bijection. On its fixed-point set, the corresponding partial normalizer belongs to $A$. On the complement, a countable separating family of measurable sets partitions the domain into countably many pieces on each of which the domain and range are disjoint. If $v$ is the restriction to one such piece, then $$v+v^*+1-v^*v-vv^*$$ is a unitary normalizer. Multiplication by its source projection recovers $v$, and strong summation recovers the original partial normalizer. Also $A$ is a MASA in $N$ and $E_A|_N$ is a faithful normal expectation, as required for the Cartan theorem.

The Feldman–Moore representation <a id="pm-cite-30"></a>[[FM77](#ref-FM77)] identifies $A$ with $L^\infty(V,\mu)$, where $\mu$ is the probability measure given by $\varphi|_A$, and identifies $N$ with the algebra of a countable nonsingular equivalence relation, allowing a scalar unitary cocycle. We use the representation on square-integrable columns, with the right base point integrated against $\mu$ and counting measure in its orbit. An algebra element acts by left matrix multiplication on the orbit column. In the twisted case, changing the base point changes the matrix by diagonal unitary conjugacy, by the scalar cocycle identity. The conditional expectation onto $A$ is its rooted diagonal. This is $E_A|_N$: if $F:N\to A$ is any normal conditional expectation, bimodularity gives $F(C_{\mathcal Q}(z))=F(z)$. The bounded compression net converges ultraweakly to $E_A(z)$ by Lemma <a href="#lem:compression-net" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:compression-net">4</a>, so normality gives $F(z)=F(E_A(z))=E_A(z)$.

Bounded graph partial-isometry kernels and diagonal coefficients generate $N$. Their algebraic span, including products, is strongly dense. A finite such expression has off-diagonal support in a finite union of partial-bijection graphs, and therefore in an undirected graph of uniformly bounded degree. Operator-norm bounds in the column representation give the same matrix bounds for almost every orbit. All models and cocycle identities below may be restricted to a common conull invariant set for the countably many operators involved.

Fix $x=x^*$ with $\left\lVert x\right\rVert\leq1$, and put <a id="exp:decomposition"></a>$$\label{exp:decomposition}
 a_0=E_Ax,\qquad Y=E_Nx-a_0,\qquad Z=x-E_Nx.$$ Then $x-a_0=Y+Z$, with $\left\lVert Y\right\rVert,\left\lVert Z\right\rVert\leq2$ and $E_NZ=0$. The measured theorem will control $Y$ through its relation matrices. For $Z$, a unitary dilation with a diffuse left–right kernel will provide random diagonal compressions whose moments approach a free compression law. Its norm bound will make the spectral excess state-null, giving a second quotient norm estimate. Common refinement combines the two bounds; clipping the resulting block-diagonal residual then supplies the correction in Theorem <a href="#thm:state-paving" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:state-paving">18</a>.

<a id="stmt-exp-normalizer-paving"></a>
<div id="exp:normalizer-paving" class="lemma">

**Lemma 19**. *Let $b,\sigma>0$ and let $r$ be an integer such that $$2\sqrt{2/r}+2/r<b.$$ There are sequences of $A$-partitions with $(r+1)^2$ members whose quotient partition $\mathcal P$ satisfies <a id="exp:normalizer-bound"></a>$$\label{exp:normalizer-bound}
 \left\lVert C_{\mathcal P}[Y]\right\rVert\leq6(Kb+\sigma),$$ where $K$ is the universal constant in Theorem <a href="#thm:measured-paving" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:measured-paving">12</a>.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-exp-normalizer-paving"><summary>Proof of stmt-exp-normalizer-paving</summary>

*Proof.* Bounded Kaplansky approximation, followed by norm approximation inside the generating algebra, gives self-adjoint finite graph expressions converging strongly-star to $Y$, with norms at most $3$. Subtracting their $A$-expectations makes their diagonals zero and increases the norm bound to $6$; state-norm contractivity of $E_A$ preserves convergence. For each such expression, cut down by the base set on which every incident edge in its finite-degree enclosing graph has cocycle ratio between $J^{-1}$ and $J$. These sets increase to full measure as $J\to\infty$, and the cuts converge strongly-star to the expression. A diagonal choice therefore gives self-adjoint $Y_j$ with $$E_AY_j=0,\qquad \left\lVert Y_j\right\rVert\leq6,\qquad
 \left\lVert Y_j-Y\right\rVert_\varphi^\#\longrightarrow0,$$ whose orbit matrices have bounded-degree support and bounded cocycle ratios, with the bounds allowed to depend on $j$.

Apply Theorem <a href="#thm:measured-paving" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:measured-paving">12</a> to $Y_j/6$ and separately to $-Y_j/6$, using state-error tolerances tending to zero. The rooted diagonal formula for the state says that the positive part above $Kb+\sigma$ has $\varphi$-norm tending to zero in each case. These positive parts are self-adjoint and uniformly bounded, so they belong to the null ideal of Lemma <a href="#lem:sequence-quotient" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:sequence-quotient">5</a>. The sequence $Y_j-Y$ belongs to that ideal as well. The resulting two quotient partitions, each of size $r+1$, therefore give respectively $$C_{\mathcal P^+}[Y/6]\leq(Kb+\sigma)1,
 \qquad
 C_{\mathcal P^-}[Y/6]\geq-(Kb+\sigma)1.$$ Take their common refinement. All partition projections commute, and compression is positive and unital, so both scalar bounds hold after refinement. This proves the lemma. Further finite $A$-refinement preserves the bound. ◻

</details>

We have completed the normalizer part. The remaining component has the following target, whose proof is given after the kernel and free-moment lemmas below.

<a id="stmt-prop-off-normalizer"></a>
<div id="prop:off-normalizer" class="proposition">

<a id="exp:off-normalizer-bound"></a>
**Proposition 20**. *For every $n\geq2$, there are sequences of $n$-partitions in $A$ whose quotient partition $\mathcal P$ satisfies $$\label{exp:off-normalizer-bound}
 \left\lVert C_{\mathcal P}[Z]\right\rVert\leq\frac4{\sqrt n}.$$*

</div>

<a id="pm-h-30"></a>
#### The diffuse kernel outside the normalizer algebra

We retain the faithful normal state $\varphi$, with $A\subset M_\varphi$, and the algebra $N$ generated by the groupoid normalizers of $A$. Use a standard probability model $A=L^\infty(V,\mu)$ for $\varphi|_A$. We use the standard-form realization $L^2(M)$, equivalently Haagerup $L^2(M)$, and write $\varphi^{1/2}$ for the positive vector implementing $\varphi$. In particular, $a\varphi^{1/2}=\varphi^{1/2}a$ for $a\in A$.

For $X\in M$, the commuting left and right actions of $A$ give a finite joint spectral measure $\nu_X$ for $X\varphi^{1/2}$ on $V\times V$. Our first coordinate is the right coordinate: for Borel sets $B,C\subset V$, <a id="exp:joint-measure"></a>$$\label{exp:joint-measure}
 \nu_X(B\times C)
 =\big\|1_CX\varphi^{1/2}1_B\big\|_2^2
 =\int_B E_A(X^*1_CX)(v)\,d\mu(v).$$ Both marginal measures are absolutely continuous with respect to $\mu$. The right marginal has density $E_A(X^*X)\leq\|X\|^2$; the left marginal is absolutely continuous because $a\mapsto\varphi(X^*aX)$ is normal on $A$. Disintegration on standard Borel spaces therefore gives a measurable kernel $k_X(v,dw)$ such that <a id="exp:kernel"></a>$$\label{exp:kernel}
 \nu_X(dv,dw)=d\mu(v)k_X(v,dw),\qquad
 E_A(X^*aX)(v)=\int_V a(w)\,k_X(v,dw),\qquad
 k_X(v,V)\leq\|X\|^2$$ almost everywhere, for $a\in A$. As usual, individual function identities are understood modulo null sets. The joint spectral resolution can be constructed from a bounded real coordinate generating the standard model and the commuting left and right spectral resolutions of that coordinate.

<a id="stmt-lem-atomless"></a>
<div id="lem:atomless" class="lemma">

**Lemma 21**. *If $E_N(X)=0$, then $k_X(v,\cdot)$ has no atoms for almost every $v\in V$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-atomless"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-atomless</summary>

*Proof.* Choose Borel representatives for the kernel. The set $$\mathcal T=\{(v,w):k_X(v,\{w\})>0\}$$ is Borel and has countable sections in the second coordinate. For example, joint measurability of the singleton mass follows by using shrinking neighborhoods in a compatible separable metric on the standard Borel space. The countable-section uniformization theorem expresses $\mathcal T$ as a countable union of graphs of partial Borel maps. If the conclusion fails, one such map $F:S\to V$ has a domain of positive $\mu$-measure, with $$g(v):=k_X(v,\{F(v)\})>0\quad(v\in S).$$ There is no assumption that $F$ is injective. For a $\mu$-null Borel set $B\subset V$, absolute continuity of the left marginal gives $$\int_{S\cap F^{-1}(B)}g(v)\,d\mu(v)
 \leq\nu_X(V\times B)=0.$$ Thus $F$ pulls back null sets to null sets. With $e=1_S\in A$, composition with $F$ consequently defines a normal $*$-homomorphism $$\theta:A\longrightarrow Ae,\qquad
 \theta(a)(v)=a(F(v))\quad(v\in S),\qquad \theta(1)=e.$$ The graph will intertwine $A$ with the possibly proper subalgebra $\theta(A)$ of $Ae$; its polar partial isometry need not yet normalize $A$. We will use an expected type I intermediate algebra to split the right support into corners where these two abelian algebras coincide.

<a id="exp:graph-intertwining"></a>
Apply the joint spectral projection of the graph of $F$ to $X\varphi^{1/2}$ and call the resulting vector $\zeta$. It is nonzero and satisfies $$\label{exp:graph-intertwining}
 \zeta e=\zeta,\qquad a\zeta=\zeta\theta(a)\quad(a\in A).$$ Its right spectral density on $S$ is $g$. In particular, $e$ is its smallest right supporting projection among the projections of $A$: if $f\in A$ is a projection and $\zeta f=\zeta$, then $e\leq f$.

<a id="exp:polar-intertwining"></a>
Take the $L^2$ polar decomposition $\zeta=u h$, where $h=|\zeta|\in L^2(M)_+$ and $u\in M$ is a partial isometry. Put $p=u^*u\leq e$ and $q=uu^*$. We use here the usual polar decomposition and support properties of standard-form noncommutative $L^2$; see, for example, <a id="pm-cite-31"></a>[[Hiai20](#ref-Hiai20), Lemma 11.12 and Theorem 11.30]. For a unitary $a\in A$, the operator $b=\theta(a)+1-e$ is unitary and <a href="#exp:graph-intertwining" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="exp:graph-intertwining">[exp:graph-intertwining]</a> reads $a\zeta=\zeta b$. Taking absolute values gives $h=b^*hb$. Uniqueness of the polar decomposition then gives $au=ub=u\theta(a)$. Consequently, $$\label{exp:polar-intertwining}
 h\theta(a)=\theta(a)h,\qquad
 p\theta(a)=\theta(a)p,\qquad
 au=u\theta(a)\quad(a\in A),\qquad q\in A.$$ The identities for all $a$ follow by linearity from unitaries. For the last assertion, the identities imply that $q$ commutes with $A$, and $A$ is maximal abelian.

<a id="exp:abelian-corner"></a>
<a id="exp:center"></a>
Consider the von Neumann algebra $$D=\theta(A)'\cap eMe.$$ It contains $p$ and $Ae$, and $Ae$ is a MASA in $D$. Moreover $\theta(A)\subset Z(D)$. If $d\in pDp$, then $udu^*$ commutes with $Aq$, by <a href="#exp:polar-intertwining" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="exp:polar-intertwining">[exp:polar-intertwining]</a>, and hence belongs to $Aq$. On the other hand, $$u\theta(a)p u^*=aq\quad(a\in A).$$ Conjugation by $u$ therefore identifies $pDp$ with $Aq$, and $$\label{exp:abelian-corner}
 pDp=\theta(A)p.$$ Let $c$ be the central support of $p$ in $D$. Since $Z(D)\subset Ae$, the identity $pc=p$ gives $\zeta c=\zeta$; the minimal right $A$-support property gives $c=e$. Thus $D$ has an abelian projection with full central support and is type I. Finally, if $d\in Z(D)$, then <a href="#exp:abelian-corner" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="exp:abelian-corner">[exp:abelian-corner]</a> gives $dp=\theta(a)p$ for some $a\in A$. The central element $d-\theta(a)$ annihilates a projection of full central support and is zero. We have proved $$\label{exp:center}
 Z(D)=\theta(A).$$

<a id="exp:branch-partition"></a>
We next need a partition of $e$ by projections $e_j\in Ae$ such that $$\label{exp:branch-partition}
 (Ae)e_j=\theta(A)e_j.$$ The restriction of $E_A$ to $D$ is a normal conditional expectation onto $Ae$. The type I MASA theorem of Akemann and Sherman <a id="pm-cite-32"></a>[[AS12](#ref-AS12), Theorem 4.1] supplies a partition of $e$ by projections in $Ae$ that are abelian in $D$. There are only countably many nonzero members, because $D$ has the faithful normal state $\varphi|_D/\varphi(e)$. For an abelian projection in a type I algebra, $$e_jDe_j=Z(D)e_j.$$ This follows, for instance, by writing the type I algebra as a direct integral: an abelian projection has fiber rank at most one. Together with <a href="#exp:center" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="exp:center">[exp:center]</a> and $\theta(A)\subset Ae\subset D$, this proves <a href="#exp:branch-partition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="exp:branch-partition">[exp:branch-partition]</a>.

Set $\eta_j=\zeta e_j$ and take its polar decomposition $\eta_j=u_j h_j$. Its right support is at most $e_j$. Repeating <a href="#exp:polar-intertwining" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="exp:polar-intertwining">[exp:polar-intertwining]</a> shows that $h_j$ commutes with $\theta(A)$ and that $$au_j=u_j\theta(a)\quad(a\in A).$$ By <a href="#exp:branch-partition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="exp:branch-partition">[exp:branch-partition]</a> and the support condition, $h_j$ commutes with all of $A$. Therefore the right support $p_j=u_j^*u_j$ belongs to $A$; the left support $q_j=u_ju_j^*$ belongs to $A$ by the same polar argument as before. In addition, $$\theta(A)p_j=Ap_j,\qquad
 u_j(Ap_j)u_j^*=Aq_j.$$ Thus each nonzero $u_j$ is a groupoid normalizer and belongs to $N$.

Let $\psi_j$ be the normal positive functional whose positive implementing vector is $h_j$. Since $h_j$ commutes with $A$, this functional centralizes $A$. In particular $\psi_j\circ C_{\mathcal Q}=\psi_j$ for every finite partition $\mathcal Q$ in $A$. Lemma <a href="#lem:compression-net" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:compression-net">4</a>, normality, and boundedness of the compression net imply $$\psi_j=\psi_j\circ E_A.$$ Write $f_j=d(\psi_j|_A)/d\mu\in L^1(V,\mu)_+$ and $f_{j,m}=\min(f_j,m)$. The positive vectors $f_{j,m}^{1/2}\varphi^{1/2}$ belong to $A\varphi^{1/2}$ and converge in $L^2$; indeed their squared pairwise distances are $$\int_V\big|f_{j,m}^{1/2}-f_{j,n}^{1/2}\big|^2\,d\mu.$$ Their normal positive functionals are $x\mapsto\int f_{j,m}E_A(x)\,d\mu$ and converge in norm to $\psi_j$. The limit is therefore the unique positive implementing vector $h_j$. It follows that $$h_j\in\overline{A\varphi^{1/2}},\qquad
 \eta_j\in\mathcal H_N:=\overline{N\varphi^{1/2}}.$$ The right projections $e_j$ are orthogonal and sum to $e$, so $\zeta=\sum_j\zeta e_j\in\mathcal H_N$, with convergence in $L^2$.

The closed space $\mathcal H_N$ reduces both the left and right actions of $A$: for the right action, use $n\varphi^{1/2}a=na\varphi^{1/2}$. It therefore reduces their joint spectral projections, including the graph projection defining $\zeta$. But $E_N(X)=0$ gives, for $n\in N$, $$\varphi(n^*X)=\varphi(n^*E_N(X))=0,$$ so $X\varphi^{1/2}\perp\mathcal H_N$. Thus $\zeta\perp\mathcal H_N$, contradicting $\zeta\ne0$ and the membership just proved. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-31"></a>
###### Removing atomic summands.

For the remaining treatment of $Z=x-E_N(x)$ we may assume that $A$ is diffuse. Here are the details of this reduction. If $f$ is an atom of $A$, then $Af=\mathbb C f$ is a MASA in $fMf$, so $fMf=\mathbb C f$. Thus $f$ is minimal in $M$. Let $z_f$ be its central support in $M$. The algebra $z_fM$ is type I, since $f$ is an abelian projection with full central support there. Compression by $f$ is faithful on its center and has scalar range, so $z_fM$ is a type I factor.

The algebra $Az_f$ is a MASA in this factor and has the normal expectation $E_A|_{z_fM}$. Again by <a id="pm-cite-33"></a>[[AS12](#ref-AS12), Theorem 4.1], it has a partition of $z_f$ into rank-one projections. In particular it is purely atomic. Partial isometries joining any two of these rank-one projections normalize their $A$-corners. A compatible system of these matrix units generates $z_fM$, and hence $$z_fM\subset N.$$ Distinct central supports of atoms are either equal or orthogonal, because each supports a factor summand. Their sum $z_{\mathrm{at}}$ is a central projection, belongs to $A$, and satisfies $z_{\mathrm{at}}M\subset N$. Consequently $$z_{\mathrm{at}}Z=0.$$ On the complementary central summand, $A(1-z_{\mathrm{at}})$ has no atoms. The algebra generated there by its groupoid normalizers is $N(1-z_{\mathrm{at}})$: corner normalizers extend by zero, and global normalizers restrict to this central summand. We may use the normalized restriction of $\varphi$ on the nonzero complementary summand. This changes state seminorms only by a fixed scalar factor. A partition obtained there extends to $M$ with the same number of cells by adding $z_{\mathrm{at}}$ to one cell; all estimates for $Z$ are preserved because $Z$ vanishes on $z_{\mathrm{at}}$.

<a id="pm-h-32"></a>
#### Random diagonal unitaries and free moments

It remains to compress the diffuse summand of $Z$. The next two lemmas supply the free law and its norm bound for the matrix dilation used to prove Proposition <a href="#prop:off-normalizer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:off-normalizer">20</a>. The state in the probabilistic argument need not be tracial.

<a id="stmt-lem-random-freeness"></a>
<div id="lem:random-freeness" class="lemma">

**Lemma 22**. *Let $\mathcal M$ have separable predual, let $\phi_0$ be a faithful normal state, and let $D\subset\mathcal M_{\phi_0}$ be a diffuse MASA. Suppose that $U\in\mathcal M$ is a self-adjoint unitary with $\phi_0(U)=0$, and that the normal positive map $$\mathcal K(a)=E_D(UaU),\qquad a\in D,$$ has an almost everywhere diffuse kernel on a standard probability model of $(D,\phi_0|_D)$. For every integer $n\geq2$ there are unitaries $w_l\in D$, with $w_l^n=1$, such that the joint star moments of $(w_l,U)$ converge to those of free unitaries $(w,U_{\mathrm f})$, where $w$ is uniform on the $n$th roots of unity and $U_{\mathrm f}$ is uniform on $\{-1,1\}$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-random-freeness"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-random-freeness</summary>

<a id="exp:small-cells"></a>
*Proof.* Choose an increasing sequence of finite partitions $\mathcal Q$ in $D$ which generates $D$, separates points of a conull standard model, and satisfies $$\label{exp:small-cells}
 \max_{q\in\mathcal Q}\phi_0(q)\longrightarrow0.$$ Such a sequence is obtained by adjoining a countable separating family and, at each stage, a sufficiently small-mass finite refinement; the latter exists by diffuseness. Give its cells independent labels $Z_q$ uniform on the $n$th roots of unity, and put $w_{\mathcal Q}=\sum_q Z_q q$.

We first make the averaged reduced moments vanish by estimating return probabilities for diffuse kernels. We then control their fluctuations using the small masses of the partition cells, so that one deterministic labeling works for each prescribed finite list of moments.

<a id="pm-h-33"></a>
###### Averaged words and a repeated-cell compression.

<a id="exp:reduced-test-word"></a>
<a id="exp:partition-word-sum"></a>
We first prove that the label expectation tends to zero for each scalar $$\label{exp:reduced-test-word}
 F_{\mathcal Q}
 =\phi_0(x_0 w_{\mathcal Q}^{k_1}x_1\cdots
                 w_{\mathcal Q}^{k_h}x_h),
 \qquad h\geq1,\quad 1\leq k_j<n,$$ where $x_j=U$ for $1\leq j<h$ and each endpoint $x_0,x_h$ is either $1$ or $U$. Expand over cell assignments $q_1,\ldots,q_h$. An assignment survives label averaging only if, in every block of equal cells, the sum of its exponents is zero modulo $n$. Consequently every block of its exact equality pattern has size at least two. For any surviving exact pattern, inclusion-exclusion expresses its word sum as a finite linear combination of sums $$\label{exp:partition-word-sum}
 S_\pi
 =\sum_{\substack{q_1,\ldots,q_h\in\mathcal Q\\
                    q_i=q_j\text{ if }i,j\text{ are in one block of }\pi}}
      \phi_0(x_0q_1x_1\cdots q_hx_h),$$ where every block of $\pi$ has at least two members. There is no distinctness requirement between different blocks in <a href="#exp:partition-word-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="exp:partition-word-sum">[exp:partition-word-sum]</a>. It is enough to show $S_\pi\to0$ for each such $\pi$.

<a id="exp:haar-compression"></a>
Choose positions $c<d$ in one block whose distance $d-c$ is minimal among all pairs in a common block. The positions strictly between them belong to distinct other blocks. We impose the equalities of $\pi$ using auxiliary Haar phases. For a block other than the selected block, place independently sampled cellwise Haar diagonal unitaries at all but one of its occurrences, and their inverse product at the remaining occurrence. Haar averaging forces precisely the equality of the cells at those occurrences. In the selected block, use the same projection $q$ explicitly at $c$ and $d$ and sum over $q$; put independent Haar diagonals at every other occurrence of that block, with their inverse product additionally at $c$. All initial draws are independent. Expanding and averaging now gives exactly $S_\pi$ as the auxiliary expectation of $$\label{exp:haar-compression}
 \phi_0\bigl(\mathrm{PRE}\,C_{\mathcal Q}(Y_{\mathcal Q})\,
                    \mathrm{POST}\bigr),\qquad
 Y_{\mathcal Q}=U d_{c+1}U\cdots d_{d-1}U.$$ When $d=c+1$, the last expression is $U$. The extra inverse product at $c$ can be moved before its projection, since both lie in $D$, and is included in $\mathrm{PRE}$. The factors outside the compression are products of fixed contractions $x_j$ and diagonal unitaries. Each interior $d_j$ is a cellwise Haar diagonal: this is also true if it is the inverse product placed at the last occurrence of its block. The interior diagonals are mutually independent because their positions belong to different blocks. Independence from $\mathrm{PRE}$ and $\mathrm{POST}$ is not asserted or needed.

<a id="exp:coarse-square-sum"></a>
Their independence and orthogonality of the outside projections give <a id="exp:haar-square-sum"></a>$$\label{exp:haar-square-sum}
 \mathbb E\left\lVert C_{\mathcal Q}(Y_{\mathcal Q})\right\rVert_{\phi_0}^2
 =\sum_{q,q_{c+1},\ldots,q_{d-1}\in\mathcal Q}
   \left\lVert qUq_{c+1}U\cdots q_{d-1}Uq\right\rVert_{\phi_0}^2.$$ There are no middle cells when $d=c+1$. Fix a coarser partition $\mathcal E$ from the generating sequence. When $\mathcal Q$ refines $\mathcal E$, contractivity of $C_{\mathcal Q}$ in the state norm, applied with each middle cell tuple fixed, bounds the right side by $$\label{exp:coarse-square-sum}
 \sum_{p\in\mathcal E}
 \sum_{q_{c+1},\ldots,q_{d-1}\in\mathcal Q}
   \left\lVert pUq_{c+1}U\cdots q_{d-1}Up\right\rVert_{\phi_0}^2.$$

<a id="pm-h-34"></a>
###### Diffuse kernel return probabilities.

<a id="exp:kernel-return"></a>
Here is the limit of this expression without a tracial assumption. Put $m=d-c$. For each $p\in\mathcal E$ define $$B_{1,\mathcal Q}(p)=UpU,\qquad
 B_{j+1,\mathcal Q}(p)=U C_{\mathcal Q}(B_{j,\mathcal Q}(p))U.$$ Expanding the squares in <a href="#exp:coarse-square-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="exp:coarse-square-sum">[exp:coarse-square-sum]</a> and summing the middle cells identifies that expression with $\sum_{p\in\mathcal E}\phi_0(pB_{m,\mathcal Q}(p)p)$. All $B_{j,\mathcal Q}(p)$ are positive contractions. By Lemma <a href="#lem:compression-net" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:compression-net">4</a>, $C_{\mathcal Q}(t)\to E_D(t)$ strongly-star on each fixed bounded element. Its contractivity for both state seminorms also controls a bounded, varying, strong-star null error. Bounded strong-star continuity of multiplication by the fixed $U$ therefore proves recursively that $B_{j,\mathcal Q}(p)$ converges strongly-star to $B_j(p)$, where $$B_1(p)=UpU,\qquad B_{j+1}(p)=U E_D(B_j(p))U.$$ Thus $E_D(B_j(p))=\mathcal K^j(p)$. If $\mu_0$ denotes the probability measure on $D$, state preservation and bimodularity of $E_D$ show that the limit of <a href="#exp:coarse-square-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="exp:coarse-square-sum">[exp:coarse-square-sum]</a> is $$\label{exp:kernel-return}
 \sum_{p\in\mathcal E}\phi_0(p\mathcal K^m(p)p)
 =\sum_{p\in\mathcal E}\int_p(\mathcal K^m1_p)(v)\,d\mu_0(v).$$

<a id="exp:haar-square-zero"></a>
The positive normal map $\mathcal K$ is unital. Its kernel is nonsingular in the integrated sense: a fixed $\mu_0$-null set receives zero mass for almost every starting point. This follows from normality, since its indicator is zero in $D$. In particular the kernel avoids, for almost every starting point, the fixed exceptional null set on which its successor kernel is not diffuse. Integrating diffuse successor kernels consequently produces a diffuse kernel. Induction shows that each fixed iterate $\mathcal K^m$ has an almost everywhere diffuse kernel. As $\mathcal E$ increases along the separating sequence, the cell containing $v$ decreases to $\{v\}$ on the chosen conull model. Its return mass in <a href="#exp:kernel-return" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="exp:kernel-return">[exp:kernel-return]</a> therefore tends to zero for almost every $v$. It is bounded by $\mathcal K^m1(v)=1$. Dominated convergence proves that <a href="#exp:kernel-return" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="exp:kernel-return">[exp:kernel-return]</a> tends to zero. First fixing $\mathcal E$ and letting $\mathcal Q$ refine, and then letting $\mathcal E$ refine, proves $$\label{exp:haar-square-zero}
 \mathbb E\left\lVert C_{\mathcal Q}(Y_{\mathcal Q})\right\rVert_{\phi_0}^2\longrightarrow0.$$

<a id="pm-h-35"></a>
###### Nontracial state estimates.

<a id="exp:analytic-right"></a>
We next explain the state estimates that turn this into scalar moment convergence. For an entire analytic element $a$ for the modular group, the standard-form right-action identity gives $$\label{exp:analytic-right}
 \left\lVert ta\right\rVert_{\phi_0}
 \leq\left\lVert\sigma_{i/2}^{\phi_0}(a)\right\rVert\left\lVert t\right\rVert_{\phi_0}.$$ See <a id="pm-cite-34"></a>[[Hiai20](#ref-Hiai20), Lemma 8.12, Equation (8.4)], applied to $a^*$. Entire analytic elements approximate each fixed element boundedly in the state norm, for example by Gaussian modular smoothing <a id="pm-cite-35"></a>[[Hiai20](#ref-Hiai20), Lemma 2.13]. A centralizer unitary has right-multiplication bound $1$. In any scalar word consisting of fixed bounded factors and varying centralizer unitaries, replace the fixed factors from right to left by bounded analytic approximants. At a replacement step all factors to the right already have uniformly bounded right actions, and all factors to the left have uniformly bounded operator norms. The error in the scalar state value is therefore at most a fixed constant times the state-norm error of the factor being replaced. Choosing the approximants in that order makes the total error arbitrarily small, uniformly over the varying diagonals and partitions.

<a id="exp:word-expectation-zero"></a>
Apply this procedure only to the fixed factors in $\mathrm{POST}$ in <a href="#exp:haar-compression" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="exp:haar-compression">[exp:haar-compression]</a>. The compression and all remaining left factors are contractions. With the approximants fixed, the absolute scalar value is bounded by $C\left\lVert C_{\mathcal Q}(Y_{\mathcal Q})\right\rVert_{\phi_0}$, where $C$ is independent of every phase and of $\mathcal Q$. Cauchy–Schwarz in the auxiliary probability space and <a href="#exp:haar-square-zero" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="exp:haar-square-zero">[exp:haar-square-zero]</a> make its phase expectation tend to zero. The uniformly arbitrarily small replacement error proves $S_\pi\to0$. Hence $$\label{exp:word-expectation-zero}
 \mathbb EF_{\mathcal Q}\longrightarrow0.$$

<a id="pm-h-36"></a>
###### Concentration and deterministic labels.

It remains to choose deterministic labels simultaneously for the moments. Replace all fixed factors in <a href="#exp:reduced-test-word" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="exp:reduced-test-word">[exp:reduced-test-word]</a> analytically, again from right to left, and call the resulting scalar $G_{\mathcal Q}$. It approximates $F_{\mathcal Q}$ uniformly to any prescribed accuracy. Changing one label $Z_q$ changes a power of $w_{\mathcal Q}$ by a scalar of absolute value at most $2$ times $q$. Telescoping over the finitely many occurrences, each difference is thus a bounded scalar times $\phi_0(\mathrm{PRE}\,q\,\mathrm{POST})$. Cauchy–Schwarz gives <a id="exp:two-sided-influence"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
\label{exp:two-sided-influence}
 \left|\phi_0(\mathrm{PRE}\,q\,\mathrm{POST})\right|
 &\leq\left\lVert q\mathrm{PRE}^*\right\rVert_{\phi_0}
        \left\lVert q\mathrm{POST}\right\rVert_{\phi_0}\notag\\
 &\leq C_{\mathrm{word}}\phi_0(q).
\end{aligned}$$ The second line follows from <a href="#exp:analytic-right" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="exp:analytic-right">[exp:analytic-right]</a> for both products, including their analytic adjoints. Their modular norm bounds are uniform over all other labels, since the diagonal unitaries are in the centralizer. Independent bounded differences (or the martingale variance sum obtained by revealing one label at a time) now give $$\mathbb E\left|G_{\mathcal Q}-\mathbb EG_{\mathcal Q}\right|^2
 \leq C'_{\mathrm{word}}\sum_{q\in\mathcal Q}\phi_0(q)^2
 \longrightarrow0$$ by <a href="#exp:small-cells" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="exp:small-cells">[exp:small-cells]</a>. The uniform approximation shows that $F_{\mathcal Q}-\mathbb EF_{\mathcal Q}\to0$ in probability. Together with <a href="#exp:word-expectation-zero" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="exp:word-expectation-zero">[exp:word-expectation-zero]</a>, this gives $F_{\mathcal Q}\to0$ in probability.

There are only countably many test words. For successively larger finite lists, convergence in probability gives a partition far enough along the sequence and a deterministic labeling for which every word in the list is as small as prescribed. A diagonal choice gives $w_l$ with all those moments tending to zero. The tests include the nontrivial single powers of $w_l$, so its limiting distribution is uniform on the $n$th roots. Also $U^2=1$ and $\phi_0(U)=0$ give the symmetric two-point distribution. Centered elements of the two limiting generated algebras are linear combinations of the tested powers and of $U$, respectively; all alternating centered moments are precisely the tests above. This establishes the asserted joint free law. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-free-compression"></a>
<div id="lem:free-compression" class="lemma">

**Lemma 23**. *Let $w$ and $U_{\mathrm f}$ have the free law in Lemma <a href="#lem:random-freeness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:random-freeness">22</a>, in its reduced free-product representation. If $p_1,\ldots,p_n$ are the spectral projections of $w$, then <a id="exp:free-norm-bound"></a>$$\label{exp:free-norm-bound}
 \left\lVert\sum_{j=1}^n p_j U_{\mathrm f}p_j\right\rVert\leq\frac2{\sqrt n}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-free-compression"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-free-compression</summary>

*Proof.* Let $E_0$ be the Hilbert-space projection onto the vacuum and all reduced words not starting in the centered part of the $w$-algebra. For a spectral projection $p$ of $w$, put $t=1/n$. On this subspace, left multiplication by $p$ is the sum of the scalar action $t$ and creation by $p-t1$. The two resulting subspaces are orthogonal, and $\left\lVert p-t1\right\rVert_2^2=t(1-t)$; hence $$\left\lVert pE_0\right\rVert=\left\lVert E_0p\right\rVert=\sqrt{t}.$$ Since $U_{\mathrm f}$ is centered, it sends a word starting in the $w$-centered space to a word starting in the other factor. Therefore $U_{\mathrm f}(1-E_0)=E_0U_{\mathrm f}(1-E_0)$, and $$pU_{\mathrm f}p
 =pU_{\mathrm f}E_0p+pE_0U_{\mathrm f}(1-E_0)p.$$ Unitarity gives norm at most $2\sqrt t$. The sum of the compressions is block diagonal for the mutually orthogonal $p_j$, so its norm is the largest block norm. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-37"></a>
#### A free-product matrix dilation

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-free-compression-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-free-compression</summary>

*Proof of Proposition <a href="#prop:off-normalizer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:off-normalizer">20</a>.* By the atomic reduction following Lemma <a href="#lem:atomless" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:atomless">21</a>, it is enough to work on the central summand where $A$ is diffuse. Normalize the state on that summand and retain the same notation. Put $z=Z/2$, so $z$ is a self-adjoint contraction and $E_Nz=0$.

The ordinary two-by-two unitary dilation need not have a diffuse kernel over $A\oplus A$: already at $z=0$, its identity off-diagonal entry gives an atomic cross-kernel. We replace the second diagonal algebra by a free diffuse abelian algebra, turning the cross terms into normal functionals on diffuse spaces. This is the symmetry form of the free-product projection dilation in <a id="pm-cite-36"></a>[[PV15](#ref-PV15), proof of Theorem 5.1]: with $y=(1+z)/2$, the symmetry below is twice that projection minus the identity. The kernel and moment lemmas above supply the required freeness in the present nontracial setting.

Let $(B_0,\tau_B)$ be a diffuse standard separable abelian probability algebra and form the reduced von Neumann free product $$(P,\psi)=(M,\varphi)*(B_0,\tau_B).$$ The free-product state is faithful and normal, the two factor expectations preserve it, and its Hilbert-space representation is the reduced-word representation; see, for example, <a id="pm-cite-37"></a>[[HU](#ref-HU)]. Both $A$ and $B_0$ remain MASAs in $P$. We give the argument to make its nontracial applicability explicit. There are unitaries $v_k\in A$ tending weakly to zero, since $A$ is diffuse. They centralize $\psi$, by the expectation onto $M$. On $L^2(P,\psi)\ominus L^2(M,\varphi)$, a dense family consists of vectors of words $$x_0 b_1 x_1\cdots b_hx_h,\qquad h\geq1,$$ where $b_i\in B_0$ are centered, the internal $x_i\in M$ are centered, and the endpoints are arbitrary. To compute a matrix coefficient of conjugation by $v_k$, take another such word with initial endpoint $y_0$, and split the middle product $y_0^*v_kx_0$ into its centered part and its scalar mean. The centered part gives a reduced word with zero expectation onto $M$. The scalar part is $\varphi(y_0^*v_kx_0)$ times an expression with a fixed norm bound and tends to zero. The additional right factor $v_k^*$ in the conjugation does not change that norm bound. Conjugation is unitary on the state Hilbert space; density proves that it tends weakly to zero on the indicated complement. If an element of $P$ commutes with $A$, its vector after subtracting its $M$-expectation lies in this complement and is fixed by all these conjugations. It must be zero. The element belongs to $M$, and then to $A$. Interchanging the factors proves the assertion for $B_0$.

<a id="exp:dilation-kernel"></a>
In $\widetilde M=\operatorname{Mat}_2(P)$ use the state $\widetilde\varphi=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}_2\otimes\psi$, with normalized matrix trace. The diagonal algebra $D=A\oplus B_0$ is a diffuse MASA in its centralizer. Define <a id="exp:unitary-dilation"></a>$$\label{exp:unitary-dilation}
 U=\begin{pmatrix}z&s\\s&-z\end{pmatrix},\qquad
 s=(1-z^2)^{1/2}.$$ Since $z$ commutes with $s$, this is a self-adjoint unitary. Furthermore $E_D(U)=0$. For $a\in A$ and $b\in B_0$, direct free-product expectation gives $$\label{exp:dilation-kernel}
 E_D\bigl(U(a\oplus b)U\bigr)
 =\bigl(E_A(zaz)+\tau_B(b)E_A(s^2)\bigr)
  \oplus
  \bigl(\varphi(sas)1+\tau_B(b)\varphi(z^2)1\bigr).$$ In particular, $E_{B_0}(zbz)=\tau_B(b)\varphi(z^2)1$ because $\varphi(z)=0$. Lemma <a href="#lem:atomless" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:atomless">21</a> makes the first kernel $a\mapsto E_A(zaz)$ diffuse. The other kernels in <a href="#exp:dilation-kernel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="exp:dilation-kernel">[exp:dilation-kernel]</a> are diffuse because the base algebras are diffuse and the displayed functionals are normal. Thus the kernel of $a\mapsto E_D(UaU)$ is almost everywhere diffuse.

<a id="exp:excess-null"></a>
Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:random-freeness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:random-freeness">22</a> with $\phi_0=
\widetilde\varphi$. Let $P_{j,l}\in D$, $1\leq j\leq n$, be the spectral projections of the resulting finite-order $w_l$, padded with zero projections when necessary, and set $$S_l=\sum_{j=1}^n P_{j,l}UP_{j,l}.$$ Each projection is a polynomial in $w_l$ and $w_l^*$. Consequently the scalar moments of the self-adjoint contractions $S_l$ tend to the moments of $S_{\mathrm f}=\sum_jp_jU_{\mathrm f}p_j$ in the reduced free law. Let $c=2/\sqrt n$. Lemma <a href="#lem:free-compression" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:free-compression">23</a> gives $\left\lVert S_{\mathrm f}\right\rVert\leq c$. Uniform polynomial approximation on $[-1,1]$, applied to the continuous function $t\mapsto(\left|t\right|-c)_+^2$, gives $$\label{exp:excess-null}
 \left\lVert(\left|S_l\right|-c)_+\right\rVert_{\widetilde\varphi}\longrightarrow0.$$ This passage uses moments to obtain a state-norm estimate, not operator-norm convergence in $\widetilde M$.

The sequence $(S_l)$ belongs to the multiplier algebra of Lemma <a href="#lem:sequence-quotient" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:sequence-quotient">5</a>: $U$ is fixed and each $(P_{j,l})_l$ is a bounded centralizer sequence. Its excess in <a href="#exp:excess-null" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="exp:excess-null">[exp:excess-null]</a> is self-adjoint, so it belongs to the strong-star null ideal. Continuous functional calculus in the quotient gives $\left\lVert[S_l]\right\rVert\leq c$.

Compress by the fixed first matrix projection $e_{11}$. Write $P_{j,l}=p_{j,l}\oplus b_{j,l}$, so the $(p_{j,l})_{j=1}^n$ form a partition in $A$. The compressed sequence is $$e_{11}S_le_{11}=\sum_{j=1}^n p_{j,l}z p_{j,l}$$ in the first corner. Its quotient norm upstairs is at most $c$. Functional calculus there makes its absolute-value excess above $c$ strong-star null. These compressed elements belong to $M$, and the state on their corner restricts to $\varphi/2$; hence the same excess is strong-star null for $(M,\varphi)$. The compressed sequence is also a multiplier sequence for $M$, since its partition projections centralize $\varphi$. Its original quotient norm is therefore at most $c$. Multiplying by $2$ proves <a href="#exp:off-normalizer-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="exp:off-normalizer-bound">[exp:off-normalizer-bound]</a> on the diffuse summand. Extending the same colors over the central summand where $Z=0$ proves the full assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-38"></a>
#### Completion of the state-preserving paving argument

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-free-compression-3"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-free-compression</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:state-paving" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:state-paving">18</a>.* Choose $$b=\sigma=\frac{\gamma}{24(K+1)},$$ choose $r$ with $2\sqrt{2/r}+2/r<b$, and choose $n\geq2$ with $4/\sqrt n\leq\gamma/2$. Use Lemma <a href="#exp:normalizer-paving" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="exp:normalizer-paving">19</a> and Proposition <a href="#prop:off-normalizer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:off-normalizer">20</a>, and take the common refinement of their partition sequences. It has $R_{\mathrm{st}}(\gamma)=(r+1)^2n$ members after padding. Finite refinement preserves each quotient bound, so for $$T_j^0=C_{\mathcal P_j}(x-E_Ax)$$ we have $\left\lVert[T_j^0]\right\rVert\leq\gamma$, since the two contributions are at most $\gamma/4$ and $\gamma/2$.

Let $\operatorname{clip}_\gamma(t)=\max(-\gamma,
\min(t,\gamma))$ for real $t$, and define $$w_j=T_j^0-\operatorname{clip}_\gamma(T_j^0).$$ The quotient norm bound and continuous functional calculus show that $(w_j)$ has zero quotient image. It is self-adjoint, hence $\left\lVert w_j\right\rVert_\varphi\to0$. Also $\left\lVert T_j^0\right\rVert\leq2$, so $\left\lVert w_j\right\rVert\leq2$. The operator $T_j^0$, and therefore $w_j$, is block diagonal for $\mathcal P_j$. Since $E_Ax$ belongs to $A$, this gives $$C_{\mathcal P_j}(x-w_j)-E_Ax
 =T_j^0-w_j=\operatorname{clip}_\gamma(T_j^0),$$ of norm at most $\gamma$. These are all the assertions of <a href="#exp:state-paving" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="exp:state-paving">[exp:state-paving]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="gen:section"></a>
### Passage to arbitrary von Neumann algebras

We first remove the separability assumption from the state-preserving result. We then treat the part of an inclusion on which no normal positive functional centralizes the MASA. The two arguments will be combined using corners by projections in the MASA; these projections need not be central in the ambient algebra. The support $e$ of the centralizing functionals will carry an expected MASA, while its complement $h$ has none of those functionals. Only after choosing the finite vector tests will we split $e=f+g$, with a faithful state on the $f$-corner and a residual projection $g$ that annihilates the test vectors.

<a id="pm-h-40"></a>
#### Separable reduction

<a id="stmt-lem-separable-reduction"></a>
<div id="lem:separable-reduction" class="lemma">

**Lemma 24**. *The conclusion of Theorem <a href="#thm:state-paving" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:state-paving">18</a> holds without the assumption of separable predual, with the same partition-size bound.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-separable-reduction"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-separable-reduction</summary>

*Proof.* Let $\varphi$ be a faithful normal state on $M$, let $A\subset M$ be a MASA in its centralizer, and fix a self-adjoint contraction $x\in M$. Construct countable sets of generators as follows. Start with $\mathbf 1,x$. At each stage form the unital $\mathbb Q(i)$-algebra generated by the current set and its adjoints. For every element $t$ of this countable algebra, add $E_A(t)$ and the projections of finite $A$-partitions $\mathcal Q_{t,k}$ satisfying $$\left\lVert C_{\mathcal Q_{t,k}}(t)-E_A(t)\right\rVert_\varphi^\#<1/k,
 \qquad k\geq1.$$ These partitions exist by Lemma <a href="#lem:compression-net" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:compression-net">4</a>. Repeat this procedure countably many times, let $B$ be the union of the resulting countable algebras, and set $$M_1=W^*(B),\qquad A_1=A\cap M_1.$$

Every element of $M_1$ is the limit of an operator-norm bounded sequence from $B$ in $\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_\varphi^\#$. Indeed, Kaplansky density gives bounded strong-star approximants from the norm closure of $B$, and one can then approximate in norm by elements of $B$. On bounded sets the state sharp seminorm determines the strong-star topology, so sequences suffice here. Taking real and imaginary parts separately gives this assertion for arbitrary elements. Since $E_A$ is contractive for the state sharp seminorm and in operator norm, it follows that $E_A(M_1)\subset A_1$.

Suppose $z\in M_1$ commutes with $A_1$. Choose bounded $t_j\in B$ converging to $z$ in the state sharp seminorm, and choose one of the stored partitions $\mathcal Q_j\subset A_1$ for each $t_j$ so that its compression error is less than $1/j$. Since $C_{\mathcal Q_j}(z)=z$, contractivity gives $$\left\lVert z-E_A(z)\right\rVert_\varphi^\#
 \leq 2\left\lVert z-t_j\right\rVert_\varphi^\#
       +\left\lVert C_{\mathcal Q_j}(t_j)-E_A(t_j)\right\rVert_\varphi^\#
 \longrightarrow0.$$ Thus $z=E_A(z)\in A_1$, proving that $A_1$ is a MASA in $M_1$. It lies in the centralizer of $\varphi|_{M_1}$. The GNS representation of this restricted faithful normal state is faithful and normal, and its Hilbert space is separable because the countable set $B$ applied to the cyclic vector is dense. Hence $M_1$ has separable predual. The restriction of $E_A$ is its state-preserving expectation onto $A_1$. Applying Theorem <a href="#thm:state-paving" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:state-paving">18</a> in $M_1$ proves the assertion, including the unchanged bound on the partition size. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-41"></a>
#### The corner without centralizing normal functionals

<a id="gen:powers-stormer"></a>
We use standard form in the next argument. Write $a\xi b$ for the commuting left and right actions, with the right action of $b$ given by $Jb^*J$. Every normal positive functional $\theta$ has a unique representative $\xi_\theta$ in the natural positive cone. This cone is selfdual and is preserved by $\xi\mapsto u\xi u^*$ for a unitary $u$. Moreover, $$\label{gen:powers-stormer}
 \left\lVert\xi_\theta-\xi_\psi\right\rVert^{2}
 \leq\left\lVert\theta-\psi\right\rVert_{M_*}.$$ These standard-form facts hold for arbitrary von Neumann algebras; see <a id="pm-cite-38"></a>[[Hiai20](#ref-Hiai20), Theorem 3.12]. The inequality also appears in <a id="pm-cite-39"></a>[[Araki74](#ref-Araki74), Theorem 4(8), Equation (5.10)]. For two functionals in a non-$\sigma$-finite algebra, it can also be applied in the corner supported by $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}(\theta+\psi)$, where their sum is faithful.

<a id="stmt-lem-no-central-functional"></a>
<div id="lem:no-central-functional" class="lemma">

**Lemma 25**. *Let $D\subset P$ be a MASA such that no nonzero normal positive functional on $P$ centralizes $D$. For every normal state $\theta$ on $P$, every integer $m\geq2$, and every $\alpha>0$, there are a positive contraction $s\in P$ and an $m$-partition $(p_i)_{i=1}^m$ in $D$ such that <a id="gen:small-diagonal"></a>$$\label{gen:small-diagonal}
 \theta(s)>1-\alpha,\qquad p_i s p_i\leq m^{-1}p_i
 \quad(1\leq i\leq m).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-no-central-functional"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-no-central-functional</summary>

*Proof.* In standard form, each finite partition $\mathcal Q$ in $D$ defines an orthogonal projection $$P_{\mathcal Q}\xi=\sum_{q\in\mathcal Q}q\xi q.$$ These projections decrease under refinement to the projection onto the $D$-central vectors. A nonzero central vector $\zeta$ would give a nonzero $D$-central normal positive functional $a\mapsto\left\langle a\zeta,\zeta\right\rangle$: conjugation by a unitary of $D$ can be transferred to the commuting right action. The hypothesis therefore implies $P_{\mathcal Q}\to0$ strongly along the net of finite partitions.

Choose independent random labels $Z_q$, uniform on the $m$th roots of unity, and put $u=\sum_{q\in\mathcal Q}Z_q q$. For $1\leq k<m$, independence and $\mathbb EZ_q^k=0$ give $$\mathbb E\left\langle u^k\xi_\theta u^{-k},\xi_\theta\right\rangle
   =\left\langle P_{\mathcal Q}\xi_\theta,\xi_\theta\right\rangle
   \longrightarrow0.$$ Each overlap is real and nonnegative, by the natural-cone properties. Consequently, for a sufficiently fine partition there is one choice of labels making all these overlaps arbitrarily small. The states $$\theta_j=\theta\circ\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}(u^{-j}),\qquad 0\leq j<m,$$ have natural-cone vectors $\xi_j=u^j\xi_\theta u^{-j}$. Their pairwise overlaps are among those just considered. Equation <a href="#gen:powers-stormer" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="gen:powers-stormer">[gen:powers-stormer]</a> shows that $\left\lVert\theta_j-\theta_k\right\rVert_{P_*}$ is arbitrarily close to $2$ for every $j\ne k$.

<a id="gen:pair-separation"></a>
<a id="gen:spectral-separation"></a>
Fix a small $\eta>0$. The positive support projection of $\theta_j-\theta_k$ attains half the norm of that Hermitian functional, since its value at $\mathbf 1$ is zero. Thus the labels can be chosen so that there are projections $q_{jk}\in P$ with $$\label{gen:pair-separation}
 \theta_j(q_{jk})\geq1-\eta,\qquad
 \theta_k(q_{jk})\leq\eta\qquad(j\ne k).$$ For $0<t<1$ put $$a_j=\frac1{m-1}\sum_{k\ne j}q_{jk},\qquad
 b_j=1_{[1-t,1]}(a_j).$$ Spectral calculus and Equation <a href="#gen:pair-separation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="gen:pair-separation">[gen:pair-separation]</a> yield $$\label{gen:spectral-separation}
 \theta_j(b_j)\geq1-\frac\eta t,\qquad
 \theta_k(b_j)^{1/2}\leq\sqrt\eta+\sqrt{(m-1)t}\quad(k\ne j).$$ For the first estimate, use $\theta_j(1-a_j)\leq\eta$ and $1-b_j\leq t^{-1}(1-a_j)$. For the second, the positive-summand inequality $1-q_{jk}\leq(m-1)(1-a_j)$ gives $$\left\lVert(1-q_{jk})b_j\right\rVert^2
   =\left\lVert b_j(1-q_{jk})b_j\right\rVert\leq(m-1)t.$$ Splitting $b_j\xi_k=b_jq_{jk}\xi_k+b_j(1-q_{jk})\xi_k$ proves the claim, using the adjoint of this operator-norm estimate.

<a id="gen:effects"></a>
The pairwise separating projections need not be mutually orthogonal, so they cannot simply be added to form a partition. Sequential products give positive effects with sum at most the identity while retaining almost full mass for their respective states. Define $$V_0=\mathbf 1,\qquad V_j=(1-b_{j-1})\cdots(1-b_0),\qquad
 s_j=V_j^*b_jV_j\quad(0\leq j<m).$$ These positive elements need not be projections. They satisfy $$\label{gen:effects}
 s_j=V_j^*V_j-V_{j+1}^*V_{j+1},\qquad
 \sum_{j=0}^{m-1}s_j=\mathbf 1-V_m^*V_m\leq\mathbf 1.$$ Telescoping the products of contractions also gives $$\left\lVert V_j\xi_j-\xi_j\right\rVert
 \leq\sum_{k<j}\left\lVert b_k\xi_j\right\rVert.$$ By Equation <a href="#gen:spectral-separation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="gen:spectral-separation">[gen:spectral-separation]</a>, whenever $\eta<t$, $$\left\lVert b_jV_j\xi_j\right\rVert
 \geq\sqrt{1-\eta/t}
       -j\bigl(\sqrt\eta+\sqrt{(m-1)t}\bigr).$$ For fixed $m$, choosing $t$ small and then $\eta$ sufficiently small makes every $\theta_j(s_j)=\left\lVert b_jV_j\xi_j\right\rVert^2$ arbitrarily close to $1$.

<a id="gen:cyclic-average"></a>
Set $$s=\frac1m\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}u^{-j}s_ju^j.$$ Then $0\leq s\leq\mathbf 1$ and $\theta(s)$ is the average of the $\theta_j(s_j)$, so it exceeds $1-\alpha$ with the choices above. Since $u^m=\mathbf 1$, Equation <a href="#gen:effects" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="gen:effects">[gen:effects]</a> gives $$\label{gen:cyclic-average}
 \sum_{\ell=0}^{m-1}u^\ell s u^{-\ell}
 =\frac1m\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}u^k
       \left(\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}s_j\right)u^{-k}
 \leq\mathbf 1.$$ Let $p_1,\ldots,p_m$ be the spectral projections of $u$ for the $m$th roots, allowing zero projections. Compressing Equation <a href="#gen:cyclic-average" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="gen:cyclic-average">[gen:cyclic-average]</a> by $p_i$ gives $mp_i s p_i\leq p_i$, as required. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-gen-no-central-approximation"></a>
<div id="gen:no-central-approximation" class="corollary">

**Corollary 26**. *Under the hypotheses of Lemma <a href="#lem:no-central-functional" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:no-central-functional">25</a>, let $P$ act normally on a Hilbert space and let $x=x^*\in P$ satisfy $\left\lVert x\right\rVert\leq1$. For each $m\geq2$, the operators $sxs$ supplied by that lemma approximate $x$ strongly, with associated $m$-partitions $\mathcal P$ satisfying $$\left\lVert sxs\right\rVert\leq1,\qquad
 \left\lVert C_{\mathcal P}(sxs)\right\rVert\leq1/m.$$ Here the approximation is on any prescribed finite set of vectors, to any positive accuracy, while $m$ stays fixed.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-gen-no-central-approximation"><summary>Proof of stmt-gen-no-central-approximation</summary>

*Proof.* For test vectors $\xi_1,\ldots,\xi_d$, normalize the sum of their vector functionals and those of $x\xi_1,\ldots,x\xi_d$ to obtain a normal state $\theta$. If this sum vanishes, all tests are zero and one may take $s=0$. Otherwise Lemma <a href="#lem:no-central-functional" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:no-central-functional">25</a> makes $$\theta((1-s)^2)\leq1-\theta(s)$$ arbitrarily small. Thus $1-s$ is arbitrarily small on every test vector and its image under $x$. The identity $$sxs-x=sx(s-1)+(s-1)x$$ proves the strong approximation. Finally, $-s^2\leq sxs\leq s^2$ and $s^2\leq s$ imply $-p_i/m\leq p_i sxs p_i\leq p_i/m$. The norm of the block diagonal sum is the maximum of its block norms. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-42"></a>
#### Assembly of the three corners

<a id="stmt-gen-support-decomposition"></a>
<div id="gen:support-decomposition" class="lemma">

**Lemma 27**. *For any MASA $A\subset M$, set $$e=\bigvee\{\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}(\lambda):\lambda\in M_*^+,
                  \ \lambda\text{ centralizes }A\},\qquad h=\mathbf 1-e.$$ Then $e,h\in A$. If $e\ne0$, there is a faithful normal conditional expectation $E:eMe\to Ae$. If $h\ne0$, the inclusion $Ah\subset hMh$ has no nonzero centralizing normal positive functional.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-gen-support-decomposition"><summary>Proof of stmt-gen-support-decomposition</summary>

*Proof.* The support of an $A$-central normal positive functional is invariant under every unitary in $A$, and hence belongs to $A$ by maximal abelianness. Such a functional is faithful on its support corner, and remains faithful and centralizing upon restriction to any nonzero $A$-subprojection of its support.

Choose a maximal orthogonal family $(q_\beta)$ of nonzero projections in $A$, each subordinate to the support of one such functional. Their sum is $e$: a nonzero remainder under $e$ would have nonzero intersection with one of the defining supports, contradicting maximality. On each $q_\beta Mq_\beta$, normalize the restricted functional to a faithful normal state. The corner $Aq_\beta$ is a MASA in its centralizer. Takesaki’s conditional expectation theorem <a id="pm-cite-40"></a>[[Takesaki72](#ref-Takesaki72)], in the state formulation <a id="pm-cite-41"></a>[[Hiai20](#ref-Hiai20), Theorem 6.6], gives a faithful normal expectation $E_\beta:q_\beta Mq_\beta\to Aq_\beta$. Its hypotheses hold because the centralizer is fixed by the modular group and a state is finite on the subalgebra.

Define <a id="gen:glued-expectation"></a>$$\label{gen:glued-expectation}
 E(z)=\sum_\beta E_\beta(q_\beta zq_\beta),\qquad z\in eMe.$$ The orthogonal block sum is well defined, bounded in norm by $\left\lVert z\right\rVert$, and belongs to $Ae$. It is positive, unital, $Ae$-bimodular, and fixes $Ae$. For a bounded increasing net of positive elements, normality of every $E_\beta$ identifies the supremum of every $q_\beta$-block; this proves normality of $E$, even for an uncountable family. If $z\geq0$ and $E(z)=0$, faithfulness on each corner gives $q_\beta zq_\beta=0$, hence $z^{1/2}q_\beta=0$ for all $\beta$. Since $\sum_\beta q_\beta=e$, this forces $z=0$. Thus $E$ is faithful.

An $Ah$-central normal positive functional on $hMh$ would extend by $z\mapsto hzh$ to an $A$-central one on $M$ supported under $h$. The definition of $e$ excludes a nonzero such functional. All corner MASAs used here remain maximal abelian: an element in a corner commuting with the corner of $A$, extended by zero, commutes with $A$. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-gen-support-decomposition-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-gen-support-decomposition</summary>

<a id="gen:final-size"></a>
*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* If $x=0$, take $y=a=0$ and the one-part partition, padded with zero projections to the size chosen below. Otherwise, by scaling it suffices to prove the assertion for $\left\lVert x\right\rVert=1$, with an arbitrary strong-approximation tolerance $\delta>0$. Fix $0<\varepsilon<1$ and put $\gamma=\varepsilon/4$. Let $r_f$ be the partition-size bound $R_{\mathrm{st}}(\gamma)$ in Theorem <a href="#thm:state-paving" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:state-paving">18</a>, and fix $m\geq2$ with $1/m\leq\gamma$. We shall use $$\label{gen:final-size}
 R(\varepsilon)=r_f+m+1.$$ More explicitly, the choices in the state-paving proof may be taken as $$b=\sigma=\frac{\varepsilon}{96(K+1)},\qquad
 r=\left\lceil\frac{32}{b^2}\right\rceil,\qquad
 n=\left\lceil\frac{1024}{\varepsilon^2}\right\rceil,\qquad
 m=\left\lceil\frac4\varepsilon\right\rceil.$$ Here $2\sqrt{2/r}+2/r\leq b/2+b^2/16<b$, $4/\sqrt n\leq\gamma/2$, and $1/m\leq\gamma$. Consequently $r_f=(r+1)^2n$ and <a id="gen:sixth-power"></a>$$\label{gen:sixth-power}
 R(\varepsilon)=(r+1)^2n+m+1
       \leq C_{\mathrm{ap}}\varepsilon^{-6}
       \qquad(0<\varepsilon<1)$$ for a universal constant $C_{\mathrm{ap}}$. Only the universal buffer $K$ enters this constant; graph, slab, and approximation parameters do not enter the color count. These choices precede the finite test set and its tolerance.

Let $e,h$ and $E$ be as in Lemma <a href="#gen:support-decomposition" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="gen:support-decomposition">27</a>. Given a finite set $F$ of test vectors, choose a unit vector $\xi_0$ such that $\left\lVert x\xi_0\right\rVert>3/4$, and put $F'=F\cup\{\xi_0\}$. On $Ae$ consider the normal positive functional $$\psi(a)=\sum_{\xi\in F'}\left\langle a e\xi,e\xi\right\rangle,\qquad a\in Ae,$$ and let $f=\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}(\psi)\in Ae$ and $g=e-f$. The equality $\psi(g)=0$ implies $g\xi=0$ for every $\xi\in F'$. Empty corners below are interpreted as zero.

If $f\ne0$, then $\psi|_{Af}$ is faithful. After normalizing it, compose it with the restriction $E:fMf\to Af$ to obtain a faithful normal state $\varphi_f$ on $fMf$. Bimodularity of $E$ and commutativity of $Af$ show that $Af$ is in the $\varphi_f$-centralizer. Apply Theorem <a href="#thm:state-paving" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:state-paving">18</a> and Lemma <a href="#lem:separable-reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:separable-reduction">24</a> to the self-adjoint contraction $x_f=fxf$. They give $$y_f=x_f-w,\qquad w=w^*,\quad\left\lVert w\right\rVert\leq2,
 \qquad
 \left\lVert C_{\mathcal P_f}(y_f)-d_f\right\rVert\leq\gamma,
 \quad d_f=E(x_f),$$ where $\mathcal P_f$ is an $r_f$-partition of $f$, and $w$ can be chosen arbitrarily small on the vectors $f\xi$ for $\xi\in F'$. Indeed, the bounded self-adjoint corrections in that theorem have state norm tending to zero, hence converge strongly in the given normal representation.

On $g$ put $y_g=0$. On $h$ put $x_h=hxh$ and use Corollary <a href="#gen:no-central-approximation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="gen:no-central-approximation">26</a> to choose $y_h=sx_hs$ arbitrarily close to $x_h$ on the vectors $h\xi$, $\xi\in F'$, together with an $m$-partition $\mathcal P_h$ of $h$ such that $$\left\lVert y_h\right\rVert\leq1,\qquad\left\lVert C_{\mathcal P_h}(y_h)\right\rVert\leq\gamma.$$

<a id="gen:norm-control"></a>
<a id="gen:test-control"></a>
Keep all off-diagonal blocks of $x$ between $f,g,h$, and replace its diagonal blocks by the operators just constructed. Explicitly, set $$y=y_f+y_g+y_h+
       \sum_{\substack{b,c\in\{f,g,h\}\\b\ne c}}bxc.$$ This operator is self-adjoint. The difference $y-x$ is block diagonal: its $f$-block has norm at most $2$, its $g$-block has norm at most $1$, and its $h$-block has norm at most $2$. Hence $$\label{gen:norm-control}
 \left\lVert y-x\right\rVert\leq2,\qquad\left\lVert y\right\rVert\leq3.$$ The $g$-block of the difference annihilates $F'$. Choosing the other two approximation errors sufficiently small therefore ensures $$\label{gen:test-control}
 \left\lVert(y-x)\xi\right\rVert<\min\{\delta,1/4\}\qquad(\xi\in F').$$ In particular $\left\lVert y\right\rVert>1/2$, by applying this inequality to $\xi_0$.
